# METHODS.md: Formulas, definitions, sources

Source-status markers:
- **[B]** Checked in the primary source.
- **[İ]** Bibliographic record verified in the reference list of a secondary source.
- **[P]** Verified from package documentation (16 Nov 2026, user decision): the equation form was taken from the help page of an R/CRAN package; the package name, version and date of reading are recorded, and the original paper stays separately as [D]/[İ]/[B]. Conditions: (1) the package has actually been introduced in a standard or peer-reviewed publication (e.g. *Journal of Statistical Software*, *The R Journal*); (2) the help page states the equation and the parameterisation explicitly (a form derived only from the code does not count as [P]); (3) the tool's independent test reference is that package's output (`tests/ref/*.R`, run by the user, output committed). Package code is not copied into the site (licence); coefficient/data tables from the package are not embedded (K1). If any condition fails, the primary source (PDF) is required.
- **[A]** Cited via a secondary source (16 Nov 2026, user decision): the equation was taken from a secondary source that cites the original source; the citation is written in two layers: "Hegyi (1974), cited in: Contreras et al. (2011)"; the original record is [İ]. Conditions: (1) the secondary source is a peer-reviewed paper or a recognised textbook (not a thesis, report or blog); (2) it states the equation explicitly with parameter definitions and cites the original source; (3) two independent secondary sources give the same form, or a single source agrees numerically with a package output ([P]) or with the source's worked example; (4) if the sources diverge, the difference becomes an option or an explicit note in the tool, or the original PDF is consulted.
- **[D]** Not yet verified; the record was written from memory, **must be verified before use**.
- **Exception: original not obtainable** (22 Nov 2026, user decision): six originals the user could not obtain are no longer requested. Each is cited only as the origin of a form or as background; the computation rests on the source actually read, named at each place, and the original keeps its record status with the note "original not obtainable". Gove and Patil 1998 (§12 P19: m₃ = d̄_G·Dq² as given by Fonseca et al. 2009 [B]); Schumacher 1939 (§15, §18: the form from Hernández-Cuevas et al. 2018 [B], Bailey and Clutter 1974 [B]); Wellek 2003 (§7: the paired equivalence test as given by Robinson and Froese 2004 [B]); Pnevmaticos and Mann 1972 (§19: background only; Grondin's form is implemented); Hui and von Gadow 2002 (§16: the optional standard angle 360°/(k + 1), labelled unverified; the default 360°/k is Pommerening 2002 [B]); Donnelly 1978 (§16: the expectation as in spatstat [P], constant 0.0412).

Numerical verification: each tool's `tests/<tool>/test_*.mjs` test (references in `tests/ref/`); for legacy code `node legacy/test_math.js`. Browser tests `tests/*/e2e.mjs`.

---

## 1. Stand measures (old `mescere.php`, archive)
**Archive (9 Nov 2026):** the old page was removed. N, G and mean diameters are in Module 2 (§6). Top height, Näslund height prediction and volume with user coefficients will be rewritten in later modules; the definitions below will then be re-verified against an independent reference.
| Quantity | Definition | Unit |
|---|---|---|
| Per-hectare expansion factor | EF = 10 000 / A, A = plot area (m²) | – |
| Number of trees | N = n · EF | trees/ha |
| Basal area | g = π/4 · (d/100)²; G = Σg · EF | m², m²/ha |
| Arithmetic mean diameter | d̄ = Σd / n | cm |
| Quadratic mean diameter | Dg = √(Σd² / n) | cm |
| Lorey's height | hL = Σ(g·h) / Σg | m |
| Top diameter / top height | Mean diameter / height of the 100 thickest trees per hectare (on the plot k = round(100 / EF), at least 1) (archived page; current definition §6: H0 from the 100 tallest trees) | cm, m |
| Reineke stand density index | SDI = N · (Dg / 25)^1,605 | – |
| Volume (form factor) | v = g · h · f | m³ |
| Volume (user equation) | v = b₀ · d^b₁ · h^b₂ (d cm, h m, v m³) | m³ |

Assumptions and notes:
- The top height definition (100 thick trees/ha) rests on the Assmann tradition. Source: Assmann, E. 1970. *The Principles of Forest Yield Study.* Pergamon. **[D]**
- General reference for Lorey's height, Dg and per-hectare expansion: van Laar, A., Akça, A. 2007. *Forest Mensuration*, 2nd edition, Springer. **[D]**
- Reineke, L.H. 1933. Perfecting a stand-density index for even-aged forests. *Journal of Agricultural Research* 46: 627–638. **[İ]** The original definition uses a reference diameter of 10 inches (25,4 cm); here the 25 cm common in metric practice is used.
- For volume the site **provides no coefficient or form factor**. The user enters f or b₀, b₁, b₂ from their own source; if not entered, volume is not computed. In the summary table and the CSV a "basis of volume" row shows the values used (5 Nov 2026 correction).

### Näslund height–diameter model (prediction of missing heights)
h = 1,3 + d² / (a + b·d)². Its linear form d / √(h − 1,3) = a + b·d is fitted by **ordinary least squares (OLS)** with the trees whose height was measured (h > 1,3). At least 3 measured heights are required. OLS on the linearised form is not the same as the least-squares solution on the h scale; this is an approximation. **Archive:** Module 5 (§10) fits Näslund by nonlinear least squares on the h scale; linearised OLS is used only as the starting value.
- Näslund, M. 1936. Skogsförsöksanstaltens gallringsförsök i tallskog. *Meddelanden från Statens Skogsförsöksanstalt* 29. **[D]**

## 2. Diameter distributions (old `weibull.php`; removed on 5 Nov 2026, see §8)
**Archive:** This section keeps the definitions of the old newspaper supplement for the record. The old page was removed with Module 4 (the site moved to forestbiometry.online on 9 Nov 2026); gamma, normal, lognormal and Johnson SB will be moved to the new tool in later rounds. The Weibull notation of the old page (α shape, β scale, γ location) is written in the new tool as **c shape, b scale, a location**. The old computation code remains in `legacy/tools.js` with the `legacy/test_math.js` tests (not published).

In all distributions x = diameter at breast height (cm).

| Distribution | Density | Parameters |
|---|---|---|
| Weibull (3-parameter) | f(x) = (α/β)·((x−γ)/β)^(α−1)·exp(−((x−γ)/β)^α), x > γ | α shape, β scale, γ location |
| Gamma (3-parameter) | f(x) = (x−γ)^(k−1)·exp(−(x−γ)/θ) / (Γ(k)·θ^k), x > γ | k shape, θ scale, γ location |
| Normal | f(x) = exp(−(x−μ)²/(2σ²)) / (σ√(2π)) | μ, σ |
| Lognormal (3-parameter) | ln(x−γ) ~ N(μ, σ²), x > γ | μ, σ (on the log scale), γ |
| Johnson SB | z = γ + δ·ln((x−ξ)/(ξ+λ−x)) ~ N(0,1), ξ < x < ξ+λ | γ, δ, ξ lower bound, λ range |

Sources:
- Bailey, R.L., Dell, T.R. 1973. Quantifying diameter distributions with the Weibull function. *Forest Science* 19: 97–104. **[İ]**
- Johnson, N.L. 1949. Systems of frequency curves generated by methods of translation. *Biometrika* 36: 149–176. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026: system S_B, eq. 19, z = γ + δ log[(x − ξ)/(ξ + λ − x)], z a unit normal variable)

### Estimation methods (location and SB bounds fixed)
Location γ and, in SB, ξ, λ are **fixed from the data, not estimated.** Defaults: γ = smallest diameter / 2; ξ = smallest diameter − 1 cm; ξ + λ = largest diameter + 1 cm. These are non-traditional defaults chosen for ease of implementation, not a recommendation from the literature; the user can change them.

| Method | Equation |
|---|---|
| Weibull, moments | y = x − γ; CV² = Γ(1+2/α)/Γ²(1+1/α) − 1 is solved (bisection); β = ȳ / Γ(1+1/α). Variance with n (population moment) |
| Weibull, maximum likelihood | Σyᵅ·ln y / Σyᵅ − 1/α − mean(ln y) = 0 (bisection, 10⁻¹⁰); β = (mean(yᵅ))^(1/α) |
| Weibull, percentiles | α = ln[ln(1−p₁)/ln(1−p₂)] / ln[(x_p₁−γ)/(x_p₂−γ)]; β = (x_p₂−γ) / (−ln(1−p₂))^(1/α). Sample percentiles are computed with type 7 as in R |
| Gamma, moments | k = ȳ²/s², θ = s²/ȳ (s² population variance) |
| Gamma, maximum likelihood | ln k − ψ(k) = ln ȳ − mean(ln y); θ = ȳ/k |
| Normal | μ = x̄, σ = population standard deviation (maximum likelihood) |
| Lognormal | μ = mean(ln y), σ = sd_n(ln y) (maximum likelihood, given γ) |
| Johnson SB | u = ln((x−ξ)/(ξ+λ−x)); δ = 1/sd_n(u), γ = −δ·mean(u). Closed-form maximum likelihood solution given ξ, λ. There is **no** full 4-parameter fit |

On the percentile pair: Dubey (1967) shows that for the two-parameter Weibull the most efficient pair for estimating **the scale and shape parameters jointly** is p ≈ 0,2388 and 0,9266 (rounded 0,24 and 0,93), and for the **shape parameter alone** p ≈ 0,1673 and 0,9737 (pp. 121–122 and 125).
- Dubey, S.D. 1967. Some percentile estimators for Weibull parameters. *Technometrics* 9(1): 119–129. **[B]**
- Related: Zarnoch, S.J., Dell, T.R. 1985. An evaluation of percentile and maximum likelihood estimators of Weibull parameters. *Forest Science* 31(1): 260–268. **[B]** (abstract)
- Relation of the moments to Dq (Dq² = β²Γ(1+2/α) + 2γβΓ(1+1/α) + γ²): Burk, T.E., Newberry, J.D. 1984. A simple algorithm for moment-based recovery of Weibull distribution parameters. *Forest Science* 30(2): 329–332. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026: Γ_k = Γ(1 + k/c); the second noncentral moment is the square of the quadratic mean; eqs. 5–6 give μ₂′ = a² + 2abΓ₁ + b²Γ₂)

### Goodness-of-fit criteria
| Criterion | Definition |
|---|---|
| Kolmogorov–Smirnov D | max |F̂(xᵢ) − Fₙ(xᵢ)|, on both sides of the ordered observations. Limit 1,36/√n (α = 0,05, asymptotic). Because the parameters are estimated from the same data, the limit is **approximate** (the actual test is not more conservative; no Lilliefors-type correction is made) |
| Error index | EI = Σ_classes |nᵢ − n·(F̂(upperᵢ) − F̂(lowerᵢ))|, in number of trees in the sample |
| AIC | AIC = 2k − 2·ln L; since location and SB bounds are fixed, k = 2 for every distribution |

- Reynolds, M.R. Jr., Burk, T.E., Huang, W.-C. 1988. Goodness-of-fit tests and model selection procedures for diameter distribution models. *Forest Science* 34: 373–399. **[İ]**
- Massey, F.J. 1951. The Kolmogorov–Smirnov test for goodness of fit. *Journal of the American Statistical Association* 46: 68–78. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026: d = max |F₀(x) − S_N(x)|, S_N(x) = k/N; the D⁺/D⁻ form at the order statistics is its standard computation for a continuous F₀)
- Akaike, H. 1974. A new look at the statistical model identification. *IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control* 19: 716–723. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026: AIC = (−2) log(maximum likelihood) + 2(number of independently adjusted parameters within the model))

### Descriptive statistics
For Weibull, gamma, normal and lognormal the mean, standard deviation, skewness and mode come from closed forms; the median from the inverse distribution function. For Johnson SB the moments are found by numerical integration of the inverse distribution function with a 4000-point midpoint rule, and the mode as the maximum of the density on a 2000-point grid (relative error < 10⁻⁴ against SciPy).
Shape interpretation: the density is scanned at 300 points over the q(0,001)–q(0,999) range. If the number of interior local maxima is ≥ 2, "bimodal"; if 0, "reverse J"/"J"/"U"; otherwise by the sign of the skewness ("nearly symmetric" if |skewness| < 0,15). This classification is descriptive, not a test.

## 3. Numerical algorithms
| Function | Method | Verification |
|---|---|---|
| ln Γ | Lanczos approximation (g = 7, 9 terms), reflection for z < 0,5 | SciPy, < 10⁻¹⁰ |
| ψ (digamma) | recurrence to x ≥ 6 + asymptotic series | Via the gamma maximum likelihood test |
| Regularised incomplete gamma P(a, x) | Series (x < a+1) / Lentz continued fraction | Gamma inverse function < 10⁻⁹ |
| Φ | Numerical Recipes `erfcc` (relative error < 1,2·10⁻⁷) | KS and log-likelihood tests |
| Φ⁻¹ | Acklam algorithm (relative error < 1,15·10⁻⁹) | SciPy, < 10⁻⁸ |
| Gamma inverse function | Wilson–Hilferty start + safeguarded Newton | SciPy, < 10⁻⁹ |

- Lanczos, C. 1964. A precision approximation of the gamma function. *SIAM J. Numer. Anal. Ser. B* 1: 86–96. **[D]**
- Press, W.H. et al. *Numerical Recipes*, Cambridge University Press (erfcc, gser/gcf). **[D]**
- Acklam, P.J. (c. 2003), "An algorithm for computing the inverse normal cumulative distribution function", unpublished web note. **[D]**
- Wilson, E.B., Hilferty, M.M. 1931. The distribution of chi-square. *PNAS* 17: 684–688. **[D]**

## 4. For visual purposes only (not scientific output)
- **Stand drawings** on the front page and in the laboratory: diameters are generated from the selected distribution, heights in Chapman–Richards form with fixed example parameters (h = 1,3 + 30·(1 − e^(−0,045·d))^1,3 + noise). These parameters do not belong to any species or region; they are for drawing only.
- Richards, F.J. 1959. A flexible growth function for empirical use. *J. Exp. Bot.* 10: 290–300. **[D]**
- **Home page output showcase (16 Nov 2026; removed together with `inc/hero.php` when the home page was redesigned on 18 Nov 2026; for the record):** drawing only. Diameter distribution: 3-parameter Weibull (a = 6, b = 21 cm, c = 2,3) density and 5 cm class probabilities; height–diameter: Näslund form h = 1,3 + d²/(1,1 + 0,17d)² with fixed-seed noisy points around it; site index: family of Chapman–Richards-form curves passing through H(50) = SI (k = 0,035, p = 1,4; SI = 16, 20, 24, 28); section volume: 2 m sections of a 26 m stem drawn horizontally (shape visual only); stand structure: fixed-seed tree positions on a circular plot. The parameters do not belong to any species or region (K1); the screen reads "drawing, curves with example parameters, points simulated". (It replaced the single stem-map drawing of 9 Nov 2026.)
- The "Sample data" buttons in the Stand Calculator and the Laboratory are simulated data randomly generated from the stated distributions and are labelled as such.

## 5. Forestry Data Checker (Module 1, `assets/lab/checker/calc.js`)
The checker performs no scientific computation; it **reads, converts and scans** the data. The thresholds below are not literature values but screening defaults the user can change.

### Reading and conversion
- The **decimal separator** of input files is a user setting (`.` or `,`). On screen and in charts numbers are shown with a decimal point (English only; Turkish display with a decimal comma removed on 21 Nov 2026), and in CSV with a point, without a thousands separator (`core/i18n.js` `fmtNum`, the single shared formatter); reading (`core/num.js`) is separate from display. Thousands separators and spaces within numbers are not accepted; such values become format errors, no guessing is done.
- **Decimal evidence:** Text cells in the mapped numeric columns are counted. Those of the form `^[+-]?\d{1,3}[.,]\d{3}$` (e.g. "1,234") count as **ambiguous** and do not decide on their own. The default decimal separator in all tools and in both languages is the **point** (10 Nov 2026, user decision). If the file clearly uses a comma (e.g. "24,5"), comma is preselected; if there is no evidence, only ambiguous values, or point and comma are seen together, point is selected and the screen says "ambiguous/conflicting, point selected by default, change it if your data use a comma". An ambiguous value is never silently converted into a thousands number: a value that cannot be read with the selected separator is reported as a format error. Import support for commas continues.
- **XLSX:** Number cells are taken as numbers (no decimal setting needed). Formulas are not read, only the last value stored in the file is used; macros are not loaded. Date-formatted cells are converted to ISO dates. In identifier and code fields the cell's **displayed text** is used (2 formatted as "000" → "002").
- **Units** are a user choice: diameter cm/mm/m → cm (×1; ×0,1; ×100), height m/dm/cm → m (×1; ×0,1; ×0,01), area m²/ha → m² (×1; ×10 000). A header suffix (`cap_cm`, `alan_m2`) only gives a suggestion; units are not changed by looking at the magnitude of the values.
- **Identifiers** are text; "001" ≠ "1", "7 " ≠ "7".

### Checks
| Code | Severity | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| `num.bad` | Error | Format (definite) | Numeric field cannot be converted to a number with the selected setting |
| `req.missing` | Error | Missing | Required field empty (plot id, tree id, species, diameter; in the plot table id and size) |
| `num.nonpos` | Error | Invalid | Diameter, height or area ≤ 0 |
| `key.dup` | Error | Duplicate | Same composite key: plot + tree (+ stem) (+ period). If period is mapped, records of the same tree in different periods are not duplicates |
| `plot.dup` | Error | Duplicate | Same id on more than one row in the plot table |
| `plot.unknown` | Error | Reference | With a plot table loaded, the plot id in a tree record is not in the table |
| `area.conflict` | Error | Conflict | More than one different size for a plot in the plot table and/or tree rows (relative difference > 10⁻⁹) |
| `stratum.conflict` | Error | Conflict | More than one stratum for a plot |
| `area.none` | Error | Missing | The plot has no size in any source |
| `text.space` | Warning | Format | Leading or trailing space in id/code (trimming is suggested, not done automatically) |
| `sp.variant` | Warning | Consistency | Different spellings of the same species code apart from case and spaces; the most frequent spelling is suggested |
| `sp.change` | Warning | Consistency | The species of the same tree changes between periods |
| `xy.half` | Warning | Missing | Only one of x, y given (1.4.0, 22 Nov 2026; both columns mapped) |
| `xy.dup` | Warning | Consistency | Two different trees (tree/stem id) of the same plot and period at exactly the same point; no distance threshold is assumed (1.4.0) |
| `dbh.range` | Warning | Biological | Diameter outside [dbhMin, dbhMax]; default 1–200 cm |
| `h.belowbh` | Warning | Biological | Diameter measured but height < 1,3 m |
| `h.range` | Warning | Biological | Height outside [hMin, hMax]; default 2–60 m |
| `sl.range` | Warning | Biological | Slenderness h/d (m/m) = h / (d/100) outside [slMin, slMax]; default 15–200 |
| `plot.empty` | Info | – | Plot in the plot table without tree records; stays in the data with n = 0 |

Errors block confirmation; warnings can be accepted with a justification and are saved with the confirmed data. No record is deleted or changed automatically; correction, exclusion, acceptance and applying a suggestion require a justification and are written to the change log with old/new value. Corrections are stored as text in the source unit and decimal format and are re-read at every evaluation. **Undo (1.4.0, 22 Nov 2026):** an edit (fix, suggestion, plot value choice) can be undone from the change log while it is the last edit of its field not yet undone and the field still holds its new value; the old value is written back (the edit is dropped when the old value is the source's) and the undo is its own log entry with a reason and the number of the entry it undoes; nothing is removed from the log.

### Derived values (the `derived` section of the confirmed data)
- Basal area: g = π/4 · (d/100)² [m²], d cm.
- Per-hectare expansion factor: EF = 10 000 / A [1/ha], A horizontal plot size (m²).

These two definitions are standard forest mensuration relations; verification is done in `tests/checker/test_checker.mjs` with hand-computed values.

### Tree age (12 Nov 2026; 1.1.0)
Optional tree field `age` (years; header suggestions: age, yaş, ağaç yaşı). Read as a number; zero or negative values are errors, an empty value is missing (not counted as 0). In confirmed data `trees[].age`, unit `units.age = 'yr'`. The type of age (total, breast-height, etc.) is not asked here: the tool that uses it (Site Index) has it selected explicitly (K8). `tests/checker/test_checker.mjs` 94/94.

### Colour palette
- Okabe, M., Ito, K. 2008. Color Universal Design (CUD): How to make figures and presentations that are friendly to colorblind people. J*Fly web page. **[D]** (page read on 22 Nov 2026: the recommended set is given only as an image, Fig. 16; the hex codes used here (#0072b2, #e69f00, #009e73, #cc79a7, #56b4e9, #d55e00) could not be checked against its text; the dark-theme variants are the site's own adjustments, not Okabe–Ito colours)

---

### Species code table and printable report (21 Nov 2026, 1.3.0; no computation)
- **Species codes:** optional table typed or pasted by the user in the settings step, one "code<tab | ; | =>name" per line (`parseSpeciesNames`; lines without a code or name and repeated codes are reported, the first line counts). Codes are matched exactly after trimming (`speciesSummary`, records in use only). The summary shows code, name and records; codes without a name and table codes not in the data are reported. The approved package gets the optional field `species_names: {code: name}` for the codes that occur (absent without a table); the species codes in the records are never changed. Nothing is built in (K1). Test: `tests/checker/test_checker.mjs` (species table), `tests/checker/e2e.mjs` §2b.
- **Printable report:** one self-contained HTML file (no scripts or external files): counts, settings, species table, every issue with its acceptance reason, the change log and the approval state; A4 print styles. The browser prints it or saves it as PDF.

## 6. Plot and Stand Calculator (Module 2, `assets/lab/stand/calc.js`)
**Scope:** Fixed-area plots; every tree in a plot has the same probability of being sampled (over the whole plot, every tree above the measurement threshold is measured). Plot sizes may differ between plots. **Nested plots, plots varying with diameter and angle counts (Bitterlich)** require a separate sampling weight (per-hectare expansion factor) per tree; this tool does not handle them automatically and asks the user to confirm "fixed-area".

**Input:** `forest-dataset/1` confirmed in the Data Checker (PROJECT.md §4b) **or a directly loaded tree table** (Excel/CSV, one tree per row; version 1.1.0, 10 Nov 2026). Units: d cm, h m, A m² (horizontal), age years.

**Direct table loading** (`tableToDataset`): the user maps the columns (required: plot, diameter and a plot-size column or a single size for all plots; optional: height, age, tree no., species, period, stratum, status). Units: diameter cm/mm, height m/dm/cm, area m²/ha. Number reading as in §5 (default decimal point; Excel number cells are numbers directly). Unreadable or non-positive diameter, area, height, age; empty plot; different size or stratum for the same plot; a tree no. repeated in the same plot and period are listed **with their row number** and the table is not used (no row is silently dropped). Empty height and age are missing values. This path does not perform the Data Checker's detailed scans (thresholds, code consistency, unit suspicion); this is stated on screen.

### Plot × measurement period unit
- Each (plot, period) combination is a unit. If there is no period information each plot is a single unit; a plot without records is a **valid empty plot**.
- If there is period information, whether combinations without records were measured and found empty or were not measured cannot be told from the data. The user chooses: "measured, empty" (N = G = 0) or "not measured" (excluded from the computation). If no choice is made, these combinations enter no summary and no forest estimate is produced for that period.
- The user chooses which records are counted according to the status values (default: all). Whether a combination counts as "measured" looks at the records before the filter; a unit left without trees after the filter is a valid empty unit.
- If `stem_id` is present, each record counts as a stem (N is the number of stems); the number of distinct tree ids is also reported.

### Unit-level measures
| Measure | Definition |
|---|---|
| Per-hectare expansion factor | EF = 10 000 / A |
| Number of trees | n (counted records), N/ha = n · EF |
| Basal area | g = π d² / 40 000 (m²); G = Σg; G/ha = Σg · EF |
| Arithmetic mean diameter | d̄ = Σd / n |
| Quadratic mean diameter | Dq = √(Σd² / n) |
| Diameter standard deviation | s_d = √(Σ(d − d̄)² / (n − 1)), n ≥ 2 |
| Smallest / largest diameter | min d, max d |
| Diameter classes | Classes are half-open [lower, upper): a tree on a boundary goes to the upper class, classes do not overlap. Boundaries must be strictly increasing (equal/decreasing boundaries are rejected). Optional open upper class [b_k, ∞). Trees below the lower boundary and (if there is no open class) above the last boundary are counted separately. For each class n, N/ha, G/ha |
| Species composition | Share of tree count = n_species / n; share of basal area = g_species / Σg. The denominators are the totals of that unit and are shown on screen |
| Dominant diameter D0 and top height H0 | D0: mean diameter of the **100** thickest trees per hectare; H0 (top height): mean height of the **100** tallest trees per hectare (user definitions: 10 Nov 2026; H0 since 19 Nov 2026 from the tallest trees only, user decision; the mean height of the thickest trees is no longer given; no source reference added). On the plot k = 100 · A / 10 000 trees. **Fractional k** (user decision: weighted): trees are sorted from largest to smallest by the ranking variable; the first ⌊k⌋ trees enter with weight 1, the next tree with weight k − ⌊k⌋; trees with equal values at the boundary share the remaining weight equally (the result does not depend on row order). D0 = Σw·d / Σw (ranking by diameter), H0 = Σw·h / Σw (ranking by height). If the number of trees is less than k, all trees are used and this is reported. H0 only with **measured** heights: for the tallest trees to be selectable, all trees in the unit must have a measured height; otherwise H0 is not given. Predicted heights are not used |
| Lorey's mean height h_L (21 Nov 2026, 1.3.0) | Mean height weighted by basal area, Kershaw et al. (2016), §8.4.1, eq. 8.12, p. 230 **[B]**: h_L = Σ nᵢgᵢhᵢ / Σ nᵢgᵢ (nᵢ trees, gᵢ mean basal area and hᵢ mean height of diameter class i); here every tree is its own class, h_L = Σ g h / Σ g. Only trees with a **measured** height (estimated heights never mixed in); the number of these trees and their share of the plot's basal area are given, with a warning when the share is below 100 % (biased if they are not representative). On a fixed-area plot the expansion factor cancels. Undefined without a measured height. Between-plot summary: descriptive only (no forest estimate). Test: `make_stand_reference.py` (numpy) per plot, `test_stand.mjs` (also: class form = tree form, equal diameters give the arithmetic mean) |
| Mean height hm | Arithmetic mean of the measured heights (the "Heights" row below) |
| Age | Arithmetic mean of the trees with a recorded age; if not all were measured, of those measured (user definition). The number of trees with measured age is also given; if there is no age, undefined |
| Heights | Measured heights (n, mean, s, min, max); heights with `height_source: 'estimated'` in the data package are summarised **separately**, not merged; the number of records without height is also given. This version does not predict heights |

**Empty but valid unit:** n = 0, N/ha = 0, G/ha = 0; d̄, Dq, s_d, min and max are **undefined** (null, "–" on screen); species shares are undefined.

**Acceptance example** (`tests/stand/test_stand.mjs`): A = 500 m², d = 10, 20, 30 cm → EF = 20; N = 60 trees/ha; G = π(100+400+900)/40 000 · 20 = 0,7π ≈ 2,1991148575 m²/ha; d̄ = 20 cm; Dq = √(1400/3) ≈ 21,6024689947 cm; s_d = 10 cm.

### Descriptive summary across plots (per period)
Each valid unit is one observation; for N/ha, G/ha and plot size the number of units, mean, standard deviation (n − 1), minimum, median, maximum. The d̄ and Dq rows only from units with trees. **Not weighted by plot size, trees are not pooled; this is not a forest estimate.**

### Design-based estimation for the forest (per period)
The estimation unit is the plot: y_i is the per-hectare value of plot i (N/ha, G/ha and N/ha, G/ha by species; 0 on a plot where the species is absent). **The trees of plots of different sizes are not pooled as if they were a single "forest area"; trees are not counted as independent observations.** If the design is not specified by the user, no estimate is produced.
- **Simple random** (systematic grid approximately with this formula): ȳ = Σy_i / m; s² = Σ(y_i − ȳ)² / (m − 1); SE(ȳ) = s / √m; df = m − 1. The population of plot locations is taken as infinite, no finite population correction is applied. If the forest area A_F (ha) is entered, total = ȳ · A_F, SE = SE(ȳ) · A_F. m ≥ 2 is required. In systematic sampling there is no exactly unbiased estimator of the variance; this formula gives an approximate result.
- **Stratified random:** W_h = A_h / ΣA_h (A_h entered by the user, ha); ȳ_st = Σ W_h ȳ_h; SE = √(Σ W_h² s_h² / m_h). m_h ≥ 2 in every stratum; a plot without a stratum and a stratum without an entered area are reported as errors. Total = ȳ_st · ΣA_h.
- **Confidence limits (21 Nov 2026, 1.3.0):** estimate ± t·SE at the level the user chooses (90, 95 or 99 %, default 95 %); totals: the same limits × the area. Simple random: t from Student's t with df = m − 1 (Cochran 1977, §2.8, eqs. 2.23 and 2.24; Cochran uses the normal deviate for n ≥ 50 and Student's t below; the tool always uses t, which tends to the normal deviate). Stratified: eqs. 5.14 and 5.15 with t from Student's t with Satterthwaite's effective degrees of freedom (Cochran 1977, §5.4, eq. 5.16) n_e = (Σ a_h)² / Σ a_h²/(m_h − 1), a_h = W_h² s_h² / m_h (Cochran's g_h = N_h(N_h − n_h)/n_h without the finite population correction, consistent with the SE above; n_e lies between the smallest m_h − 1 and their sum). With SE = 0 the limits equal the estimate. The limits assume an approximately normal mean (Cochran §2.8, §2.15); with few plots and very skewed values they can be too narrow. Variance results used: Theorem 2.2 (eq. 2.8), Theorem 2.4 and its corollary (eqs. 2.20, 2.21), Theorem 5.5 (eqs. 5.12, 5.13).
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_stand_reference.py` adds the limits at 95 % and 90 % with `scipy.stats.t` (scipy 1.18.1) for both designs and both scenarios, Satterthwaite df written separately; `tests/stand/test_stand.mjs` agrees to 1e-10 (t, df, limits, limits of totals), checks n_e between min(m_h − 1) and Σ(m_h − 1) (Cochran p. 96) and reproduces Cochran's worked example (p. 28: petition sheets, N = 676, n = 50, total 19,888, s² = 229.0, 80 % limits 18,107–21,669 with the normal deviate 1.28 and the fpc; within the book's rounding of its intermediate values). `tests/stand/e2e.mjs` §3a: limits on screen = estimate ± t·SE.
- Cochran, W.G. 1977. *Sampling Techniques*, 3rd edition. John Wiley & Sons, New York. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, 21 Nov 2026: §2.4–2.8 pp. 21–28, eqs. 2.6–2.8, 2.12–2.14, 2.17–2.24 and the example on p. 28; §5.4 pp. 95–96, eqs. 5.11–5.16)

**Export appended to the tree data** (Excel, "Data" + "Info"): source table rows and values are written unchanged; to each row the D0, H0 (depending on the choice `D0`/`H0`, or `D0_d`/`H0_d` by diameter and `D0_h`/`H0_h` by height), hm, dm, N, BA, age, Dq values of its own plot × period unit are appended at full precision; if the name exists in the table, `_plot` is appended. If the status filter was used, an `in_calc` (1/0) column is added. A value that cannot be computed is an empty cell. Definitions, units and the k rule are on the "Info" sheet.

### Verification
D0/H0, age and the other parameters: `tests/ref/make_stand_dominant_reference.py` (numpy; with per-hectare stem accumulation instead of weights, separately written code) produces 49 cases: k = 12.5, 5.3, 3.15, k < 1, equal values at the boundary, tallest ≠ thickest, fewer trees than k, missing height inside/outside the dominant set, equal diameters, 40 random plots. `test_stand.mjs` compares with 10⁻¹² relative tolerance and with the row order reversed; in addition a hand-computed example: 1250 m², d = 20…39 cm → D0 = 415.5 / 12.5 = 33.24 cm. On the user's 135-plot Anatolian black pine file (not put in the repository) JS and numpy gave at most 6·10⁻¹⁶ relative difference over 1350 values.

`tests/ref/make_stand_reference.py` (numpy, separately written code) computes, on the synthetic `tests/stand/fixture.json`, for two scenarios (all records + empty combinations; live only + not measured) the unit measures, class frequencies, species shares, height counts, the across-plot summary, the simple random and stratified estimates; `tests/stand/test_stand.mjs` compares with 10⁻¹⁰ relative tolerance.

### Shared functions: contract for the new tools (T01, 12 Nov 2026)
The new tools (TOOLS_SPEC) **import** these functions **directly** from `assets/lab/stand/calc.js`; they are not moved or copied (T00 §2). Their behaviour is the definitions above; if they change, the tests of the Plot tool and the tests of the tools that use them are updated together.

| Function | Input | Output |
|---|---|---|
| `validateDataset(ds)` | `forest-dataset/1` object | array of problem codes (empty = valid) |
| `periodsOf(ds)`, `statusesOf(ds)` | same | periods (null = no period; numeric periods sorted numerically), status values |
| `buildUnits(ds, { status, emptyCombos })` | same; statuses to include; meaning of a plot–period combination without trees | `{ units: [{ plot_id, period, area (m²), stratum, state: 'measured'\|'empty'\|'unmeasured'\|'unknown', nRecords, trees }], periods, noPeriod }` |
| `unitMetrics(u, classes?)` | one unit (only `measured`/`empty`), optional classes | `{ ef = 10000/A, n, Nha, G_m2, Gha, d_mean, dq, d_min, d_max, d_sd, classes?, species, h_measured, h_estimated, h_missing, age, dom: { k, d, h } }` |
| `dominantWeights(trees, k, rank)`, `dominant(trees, k, rank)` | trees, k = 100·A/10000, `'dbh'` or `'height'` (measured height only) | per-tree weights (fractional k, sharing among ties) or null; weighted mean and `short` flag |
| `classesFromBreaks`, `breaksFromWidth`, `classIndex` | boundary array / start, width, end / classes and diameter | half-open [lo, hi) classes; −1 lower, `classes.length` upper overflow |
| `tableToDataset(tbl, map, spec, parseNumber, toInternal)` | raw table, column mapping, reading settings | `{ ds, problems }` (problems with row numbers; ds = null if there are problems) |

The limitation carries over to every tool that uses these functions: the expansion factor is only for **fixed-area** plots (no nested plots and no angle counts).

## 7. Model Performance Evaluator (Module 3, `assets/lab/eval/calc.js`; 19 Nov 2026, 1.2.0-beta.1: weighted criteria, confidence interval for bias, equivalence tests, bootstrap intervals)
**Input:** Any table (CSV/TSV/paste/XLSX) or a confirmed data package. The user selects one observed column, one or more prediction columns and optional plot, species, diameter (binned into classes) and validation fold columns. Number reading rules are the same as the Data Checker's (§5): decimal separator is a user choice (default point, comma if the file clearly uses a comma); XLSX number cells are numbers directly.

**Sign convention (in all outputs):** e_i = y_i − ŷ_i (observed − predicted; until 19 Nov 2026 it was ŷ − y, changed by user decision). Negative bias means overprediction on average.

| Criterion | Definition |
|---|---|
| n | Number of valid pairs (observed and predicted both readable numbers) |
| Bias | ē = Σe_i / n |
| MAE | Σ\|e_i\| / n |
| RMSE | √(Σe_i² / n); denominator **n** (not n − p; the number of model parameters is unknown) |
| Prediction R² | 1 − SSE/SST; SSE = Σe_i², SST = Σ(y_i − ȳ)². **Not replaced** by the squared correlation; may be negative and is **not clipped to zero**; **undefined** if all observed values are equal (SST = 0) |
| Relative criteria (optional) | Criterion / denominator × 100. The user chooses the denominator and it is shown in the table: observed mean ȳ (undefined if ȳ ≤ 0), observed range max − min (undefined if 0), observed standard deviation s_y, denominator n − 1 (undefined if n < 2 or s_y = 0) |
| Additional information | Observed and predicted means, smallest/largest e |

**Row sets:** The default is the **common valid rows**: rows where the observed value and all selected predictions are readable; the models are compared on the same sample. Optional "each model with its own valid rows": if the samples differ a warning is given and a model × model table of common rows is shown. For each model the numbers of rows with observed empty, observed unreadable, prediction empty, prediction unreadable, its own valid pairs, used and left out are reported.

**Groups:** The same criteria are computed by plot, species, diameter class (half-open [lower, upper) classes, same rule as §6) and validation fold, within the selected row set. A row with an unreadable diameter stays in the "(empty)" group.

**Source of the predictions:** The user states it for each model: not stated (default), model development data, independent test data, cross-validation. The tool does not assume external validation; for the not-stated and model-development-data cases it writes a warning. The source declaration does not change the computations, it is only reported.

**Weighted criteria (19 Nov 2026, 1.2.0-beta.1; definition [D], user decision):** The user optionally selects a weight column (e.g. sampling weight, per-hectare expansion factor). The weight w_i must be > 0; a row with an empty, unreadable, zero or negative weight is left out and counted as "weight empty" / "weight unreadable or ≤ 0" (in the common row set the validity of the weight is also required). Every mean becomes Σw_i(…)/Σw_i: bias Σw_ie_i/Σw_i, MAE Σw_i|e_i|/Σw_i, RMSE √(Σw_ie_i²/Σw_i), R² = 1 − Σw_ie_i² / Σw_i(y_i − ȳ_w)², ȳ_w = Σw_iy_i/Σw_i; the observed and predicted means are also weighted. w ≡ 1 gives the unweighted definitions. n is the number of pairs, Σw is recorded separately. In the relative criterion the mean denominator is ȳ_w, the range denominator does not change; the standard deviation denominator is undefined when weighted (no definition of weighted standard deviation was chosen here). Groups are computed with the same weights. While a weight is selected, the inference below is not computed.

**Confidence interval for bias (19 Nov 2026; standard t interval [D], user decision):** For unweighted pairs, for each model ē ± t(1 − (1 − γ)/2; n − 1)·s/√n; s = √(Σ(e_i − ē)²/(n − 1)); γ = 0,90, 0,95 (default) or 0,99. Undefined if n < 2. Independence of the pairs is assumed; the screen states that for clustered data, such as trees on the same plot, the interval may come out too narrow.

**Equivalence tests (19 Nov 2026) [B]:** Robinson, A.P., Froese, R.E. 2004. Model validation using equivalence tests. *Ecological Modelling* 176: 349–358 (PDF provided by the user, read on 19 Nov 2026; §1.1, §2.3, Equations 3, 9, 10, Table 1). The null hypothesis is dissimilarity (model biased: |μ_e| ≥ δ); if it is rejected, it is shown that the mean error lies within ±δ. The difference in the paper is x_i − x̂_i (observed − predicted); since 19 Nov 2026 the tool uses the same sign (e = y − ŷ). The user enters the limit either as δ in data units or as ε relative to the standard deviation of the errors (δ = ε·s; the paper uses both and converts absolute criteria to ε by dividing by s). α = 0,01, 0,05 (default) or 0,10.
- **TOST** (§1.1): equivalent if the interval ē ± t(1 − α; n − 1)·s/√n (two one-sided α intervals, algebraically a 1 − 2α two-sided interval) lies strictly within (−δ, δ).
- **Paired t-test for equivalence** (§2.3, cited from Wellek 2003, original not obtainable (22 Nov 2026, user decision; see the legend)): ε = δ/s, ψ² = nε²; the cutoff C is the **square root** of the α percentile of the F distribution with degrees of freedom 1 and n − 1 and noncentrality parameter ψ²; t = ē/(s/√n) (Equation 9); if |t| < C dissimilarity is rejected (note to Table 1: absolute values are compared, not rejected if |t| ≥ C). The paper's text says "α percentile"; the cutoffs in Table 1 agree with the square root of the percentile (below), the tool uses the square root. Power, when the true μ_e = 0, is 2F_t(C; n − 1) − 1 (Equation 10); the paper calls F_t the "noncentral t distribution"; since at μ_e = 0 the noncentrality parameter is 0, the tool uses the central t distribution (the powers 0,11 and 0,15 in Table 1 agree with this).
- **Numerical method:** P(|T| ≤ c), T ~ noncentral t(n − 1, √ψ²), i.e. P(F ≤ c²): the expectation E_V[Φ(c√(V/ν) − δ') − Φ(−c√(V/ν) − δ')] over V ~ χ²_ν with composite Gauss–Legendre in the variable x = ln V (400 intervals × 16 nodes, tails where the density stays below e⁻⁴⁰ of its peak excluded); C by Brent's method (`core/stats.js` `absNcTCdf`, `ncF1Quantile`). Identical to R `qf(α, 1, ν, ncp)` within 1e-8 relative over n = 2…10⁶.
- **Undefined cases:** n < 2; s = 0 (ε undefined); limit not a positive number.
- **Comparison with the source (Table 1, α = 0,01):** n = 3393 (diameter; s = 1,9 cm) and n = 549 (volume; s = 10,6 m³/ha). For relative criteria (ε = 0,10, 0,25, 0,50) the cutoffs 3,50; 12,21; 0,19; 3,52; 9,30 are reproduced exactly to two decimals. For absolute criteria (0,25 and 0,50 cm; 1 and 5 m³/ha), with the rounded s, 5,33; 12,97; 0,14; 8,65 are obtained (paper 5,33; 12,96; 0,14; 8,69; the difference comes from the paper's unrounded s). In the diameter 50% row the paper writes 27,77; R and the tool give 26,68 (noted as a misprint since the other nine rows agree). The paper's t = −5,72 comes out as −5,82 from the rounded mean and s; the decisions do not change.
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/eval_inference.R` (base R: `weighted.mean`, `t.test`, `qf(…, ncp)`, `pt`; R 4.3.3, run in this session) writes the weighted criteria on `tests/eval/fixture.csv` (weight = diameter column), the 90/95/99% intervals, five equivalence settings and the Table 1 cutoff/power values to `tests/ref/eval_inference.json`; `tests/eval/test_eval.mjs` compares against them.
- **Out of scope (this version):** signed-rank equivalence test, per-group inference, correction for clustered data.

**Bootstrap confidence intervals (19 Nov 2026) [B]:** DiCiccio, T.J., Efron, B. 1996. Bootstrap confidence intervals. *Statistical Science* 11(3): 189–228 (§2, §6; Equations 2.2, 2.3, 2.8, 6.3, 6.6, 6.7) and DiCiccio, T.J., Romano, J.P. 1988. A review of bootstrap confidence intervals. *Journal of the Royal Statistical Society B* 50(3): 338–354 (§2.1, §2.4: percentile and BC limits, z₀ = Φ⁻¹(Ĝ(θ̂)), in the nonparametric case the acceleration from the influence function); both PDFs provided by the user, read on 19 Nov 2026. The part of Efron and Tibshirani 1993 that was provided (chapter 17) does not contain these methods.
- **Resampling (Eq. 6.3):** n (observed, predicted) pairs are drawn n times with replacement; index j = ⌊u·n⌋, u = mulberry32(seed) (`core/stats.js` `rng`, k/2³²); the same seed gives the same samples. In each sample bias, MAE, RMSE and R² (§7 definitions, unweighted). B = 1000, 2000 (default) or 5000; confidence level γ the same as for the t interval above.
- **Percentile interval:** Ĝ(c) = #{θ̂*_b < c}/B (Eq. 2.2); Ĝ⁻¹(α) is the ⌈αB⌉-th smallest replicate (the smallest c satisfying Ĝ ≥ α; DiCiccio–Romano's definition inf{x: J(x) ≥ α}); interval [Ĝ⁻¹((1 − γ)/2), Ĝ⁻¹((1 + γ)/2)]. If αB falls just above an integer because of floating-point error (e.g. (1 − 0,95)/2·2000 = 50,00000000000004), a tolerance of 10⁻⁹ is applied and the 50th is taken.
- **BCa (Eq. 2.3):** θ̂_BCa[α] = Ĝ⁻¹(Φ(ẑ₀ + (ẑ₀ + z_α)/(1 − â(ẑ₀ + z_α)))); ẑ₀ = Φ⁻¹(#{θ̂*_b < θ̂}/B) (Eq. 2.8); â = ΣU_i³ / (6(ΣU_i²)^{3/2}) (Eq. 6.6), U_i = (n − 1)(θ̂_· − θ̂_(i)) jackknife influence values (Eq. 6.7; θ̂_(i) with the i-th pair removed, θ̂_· their mean). If all replicates are on one side of θ̂, ẑ₀ is infinite; if 1 − â(ẑ₀ + z_α) ≤ 0, the correction is undefined; if the criterion is undefined when a pair is removed, there is no â: BCa is reported as undefined, the percentile interval remains. At a = z₀ = 0 BCa reduces to the percentile interval.
- **Undefined replicates:** in a sample whose observed values are constant R² is undefined; that replicate is left out for R², the number of valid replicates is reported (Ĝ is built from them). If an endpoint is taken from the smallest or largest replicate, it is marked with "*" (B should be increased).
- **While a weight is selected** the bootstrap is not computed (like the other inference). Independence of the pairs is assumed.
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/eval_boot.R` writes the method separately from the paper in base R (same generator and seed): in four cases (fixture two models B = 2000, γ = 0,95; eight-pair example B = 2000, seed 7, γ = 0,90; B = 1000, seed 42, γ = 0,99) all percentile and BCa endpoints, ẑ₀ and â are identical within 10⁻⁹…10⁻¹²; the percentile endpoints are also identical to R's `quantile(type = 1)` function; â for the linear criteria (bias, MAE) is identical to `boot::empinf(type = "jack")` (boot 1.3-30; it differs for RMSE and R² because the package centres the jackknife values around θ̂ instead of θ̂_·, Eq. 6.7 uses θ̂_·). R's `boot.ci` function was not included in the comparison because it uses interpolated ((B + 1)α) percentiles.

**Uncertainty of the ranking of the models (21 Nov 2026, 1.2.0-beta.3):** with the bootstrap on, no weight and two or more models, every bootstrap sample above (same seed → the same samples; drawn on the rows common to all models, the same rows for every model) is ranked with the relative rank of Relative Ranking (§9; Poudel and Cao 2013, Eq. 10 **[B]**, generalised by direction) on |bias| (closest to zero), MAE and RMSE (smaller) and R² (larger), equal weights; overall relative rank = Σ of the four criterion ranks rescaled to 1…m, rank number by competition ranking (1, 2, 2, 4). Reported per model: the overall relative rank on the full data and its rank number, the percentile interval of the overall relative rank over the samples (Ĝ⁻¹ as above, DiCiccio and Efron 1996 eq. 2.2 **[B]**), the mean rank number and the share of samples in which the model has rank 1 (tied models each count). A sample in which a criterion is undefined (R² with constant observed values) is left out and counted. This is a combination of the two sourced pieces; Poudel and Cao (2013) mention that ranks change across bootstrap samples, but their procedure was not taken from the paper (not read for it), so no claim is made that it is theirs. The relative-rank functions are shared with Relative Ranking (`core/relrank.js`, moved unchanged), which stays independent of this tool.
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_eval_rankboot_reference.py` writes the method separately in numpy (same generator mulberry32, same draws): sample file (2 models, B = 1000), simulated (4 models with a tie, B = 500, 90 %), tiny data with skipped samples (B = 200); `tests/eval/test_eval.mjs` agrees to 1e-12 (overall rank, rank number, interval, mean rank, share first, valid/skipped counts) and the full-data overall ranks equal Relative Ranking's `rank()`; `tests/eval/e2e.mjs` §4c: screen table and Excel sheet as calc.js.

**Not computed:** From the observed/predicted columns alone no AIC, parameter confidence interval, model significance, adjusted R² or criteria based on the number of parameters are produced (the number of parameters and the residual structure are unknown).

**Charts:** Observed (x) – predicted (y), equal axis ranges and a dashed 1:1 line; residual e (y) – predicted (x), dashed e = 0 line. Downloadable as SVG. Optionally the points are coloured by a chosen group column (plot, species, diameter class or fold; up to 18 groups: six colours, then square and triangle markers; more groups are not coloured and a note says so; 22 Nov 2026). Below the charts, the residual table of the chosen model on screen: row, group labels, observed, predicted, e (and weight), in row order or largest |e| first; at most 1000 rows on screen, all rows in the downloads. Display only: no measure changes.

**Acceptance example:** y = [10, 20, 30], ŷ = [12, 18, 33] → e = y − ŷ = [−2, 2, −3]; ē = −1; MAE = 7/3 ≈ 2,3333333333; RMSE = √(17/3) ≈ 2,3804761428; SSE = 17, SST = 200, R² = 0,915 (the Pearson r² of the same data is ≈ 0,9423; not to be confused with R²).

**Verification:** `tests/ref/make_eval_reference.py` produces references with scikit-learn (`mean_absolute_error`, `root_mean_squared_error`, `r2_score`) and numpy on the synthetic `tests/eval/fixture.csv` for the common and own row sets, groups and relative criteria. Because scikit-learn's `r2_score` function returns a finite placeholder for a constant observed variable, this case is written separately as "undefined" in the reference.

## 8. Diameter Distribution Laboratory, Weibull version (Module 4; retired 10 Nov 2026, code `legacy/weibull/calc.js`, replaced by §12)
**Parameters (the same everywhere):** a = location, b > 0 = scale, c > 0 = shape.
F(d) = 1 − exp(−((d − a)/b)^c), d > a; F(d) = 0 for d ≤ a. f(d) = (c/b)((d − a)/b)^(c−1) exp(−((d − a)/b)^c), d > a.
Mean a + bΓ(1 + 1/c); variance b²[Γ(1 + 2/c) − Γ²(1 + 1/c)]; median a + b(ln 2)^(1/c); mode a + b((c − 1)/c)^(1/c) (c > 1), otherwise a. Percentile a + b(−ln(1 − q))^(1/c). To avoid numerical loss, computed with F = −expm1(−t^c) and 1 − F = exp(−t^c).

**Input:** Individual tree diameters (cm; ×0,1 if mm is selected) and optional plot id; an optional filter (e.g. period). Empty/unreadable and zero or negative diameters are excluded from the computation and their counts reported.

**Unit of analysis:** Each plot is fitted separately (default) or all trees in a single pool. In the pool each tree gets the same weight; if the plots are not of equal size, the pooled distribution does not represent the per-hectare diameter distribution (warning on screen).

**Location a is not estimated.** In the two-parameter form a = 0. In the three-parameter form a is a value **fixed** by the user; b and c are estimated; the number of free parameters is 2. a is not taken automatically from the sample minimum. Constraints: a is a number, a ≥ 0 (tool rule: a negative location is not accepted for diameter) and in every unit of analysis a < min(d), i.e. every d − a > 0. If not satisfied, no fit is made for that unit and the reason is stated.

### Estimation methods (x = d − a)
- **Maximum likelihood:** ln L = n ln c − n c ln b + (c − 1) Σ ln x − Σ (x/b)^c. Profile equation g(c) = Σ x^c ln x / Σ x^c − 1/c − mean(ln x) = 0 (increasing in c; a single root for non-constant data), then b = (mean x^c)^(1/c). To avoid numerical overflow, computed with x divided by its largest value.
- **Moments:** The mean and the sample variance (denominator n − 1) are matched: s²/x̄² = Γ(1 + 2/c)/Γ²(1 + 1/c) − 1 (decreasing in c), b = x̄/Γ(1 + 1/c).
- **Solution and status report:** The root c is searched in the interval [0,01; 1000] by geometric bisection (relative tolerance 10⁻¹⁴, at most 300 iterations). Statuses: *converged*; *boundary solution* (root outside the interval: c > 1000, nearly constant data, or c < 0,01) — no parameters are given; *did not converge* (equation numerically undefined or iteration limit); *not fitted* (n < 2, all diameters equal, invalid a).
- **Warnings (do not stop the fit):** If the number of distinct diameters is < 10, "few distinct values" (tool threshold, not a literature value); if n ≥ 20, distinct values ≤ 15 and distinct/n < 0,2, "looks like class midpoints" (requires a grouped-data likelihood); if c ≤ 1 the density is largest at a.
- **Truncation:** No fit is made until the user answers the measurement-threshold question. Data truncated at a known threshold are not fitted in this version; the correct method is the truncated likelihood: f(d)/[1 − F(t)], d ≥ t. Setting the location equal to the threshold does not correct truncation. Data in class-frequency form (class midpoint + count) are not accepted in this version.

### Goodness-of-fit statistics (no p-value)
- KS D = max_i max(F(x₍ᵢ₎) − (i − 1)/n, i/n − F(x₍ᵢ₎)).
- AD A² = −n − (1/n) Σ (2i − 1)[ln F(x₍ᵢ₎) + ln(1 − F(x₍ₙ₊₁₋ᵢ₎))], on the log scale.
- Because the parameters are estimated from the same data, standard table critical values and p-values are not valid; since there is no verified calibration (e.g. parametric bootstrap), no p-value is given.
- AIC = 2k − 2 ln L, BIC = k ln n − 2 ln L, k = 2; only in the maximum likelihood rows (moment estimates do not maximise the likelihood). The two ML rows (a = 0 and fixed a) are comparable with the same data and the same density unit; if a was chosen by looking at the data, this choice is not reflected in the AIC. AICs obtained with different plots or different filters are not compared.

### Class frequencies
Classes are half-open [lower, upper) (same rule as §6, no overlap). The class probability F(upper) − F(lower) is computed numerically as a difference of 1 − F; the expected count is n·[F(upper) − F(lower)]. The lower tail F(b₀) and the upper tail 1 − F(b_k) are separate rows; the visible classes are not renormalised, the probabilities sum to 1 only with the tails. Histogram density = class count/(n·width).

### Curve explorer
Density, cumulative distribution, mean, standard deviation, median and percentiles with the a, b, c given by the user. No estimation is made from any data; it stands in a separate section on screen with the label "illustration, not a fit to data".

### Verification
`tests/ref/make_weibull_reference.py`: synthetic samples generated with numpy `default_rng(20261105)` (two-parameter, shifted, small sample, c < 1) and multi-plot data; SciPy `brentq` on the same estimating equations, `weibull_min` cdf/sf/pdf/ppf/mean/std, `kstest`, A² with `logcdf`/`logsf`, class probabilities; `weibull_min.fit(floc=0)` cross-checks the ML root with a loose tolerance. `tests/weibull/test_weibull.mjs` additionally tests the F limits, constant/small/single-tree samples, the boundary solution and the non-convergence report. `lnGamma` was moved unchanged from `assets/tools.js` to `assets/lab/core/special.js`; that the two implementations give the same result is tested.
- Bailey, R.L., Dell, T.R. 1973. Quantifying diameter distributions with the Weibull function. *Forest Science* 19: 97–104. **[İ]**
- Anderson, T.W., Darling, D.A. 1954. A test of goodness of fit. *J. Am. Stat. Assoc.* 49: 765–769. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026: eq. 2, W_n² = −n − (1/n) Σ (2j − 1)[log u_j + log(1 − u_(n−j+1))], natural logarithms; eq. 3, the Cramér–von Mises nω² = 1/(12n) + Σ [u_j − (2j − 1)/(2n)]², as in §12)
- Cohen, A.C. 1965. Maximum likelihood estimation in the Weibull distribution based on complete and on censored samples. *Technometrics* 7: 579–588. **[D]**

## 9. Relative Ranking (Module 7, `assets/lab/rank/calc.js`)
**Input:** A table of performance criteria the user has obtained from their statistics program (XLSX/CSV/paste): rows are models, columns are criteria; the number of criteria is free. The header row is detected automatically (the first row whose first cell is text, with at least two other text cells and numbers below it); the user can change it. It is independent of the Model Performance Evaluator (user decision).

**Criterion direction** (confirmed by the user; suggested from the column name; for an unrecognised name no ranking is done until the user chooses):
- *Larger is better:* R², adjusted R², Fit Index (FI), model efficiency (EF/NSE).
- *Smaller is better:* RMSE, MSE, MAE, MAB, AAE, MAPE, AIC, AICc, BIC, SSE, KS, AD, EI, −ln L.
- *Closest to zero is better:* signed criteria (Bias, ME, MPE…); ranked by |value|.

**Relative rank** (Poudel and Cao 2013, Eq. 10; there all criteria are "smaller is better", here generalised by direction):
Rᵢⱼ = 1 + (m − 1)(Sᵢⱼ − S_best,ⱼ)/(S_worst,ⱼ − S_best,ⱼ). For "larger is better" best = max, worst = min; for "smaller is better" the reverse; for "closest to zero" S = |value| and best = min |value|. The best model gets 1, the worst m.
**Overall relative rank:** Totalᵢ = Σⱼ wⱼRᵢⱼ over the selected criteria; Overallᵢ = 1 + (m − 1)(Totalᵢ − min Total)/(max Total − min Total). A rank number (with ties 1, 2, 2, 4) is also given. **Criterion weights (21 Nov 2026, 1.2.0; definition [D], user decision):** wⱼ > 0 typed by the user, default 1 for every criterion, which is the equal weighting of Poudel and Cao (2013); only their ratios matter (the rescaling removes a common factor). The tool suggests no weights. Weights shown with the results, in the exports and in the run record. Verification: scenario `weighted` of `tests/ref/make_rank_reference.py` (numpy; weights that change the order), `tests/rank/test_rank.mjs` (also: doubling all weights changes nothing, all weights 1 = the unweighted ranking, w ≤ 0 or unreadable refused), `tests/rank/e2e.mjs` §3b.

**Special cases:**
- If all models are equal on a criterion, the denominator is zero: everyone gets 1 on that criterion and the case is reported (does not change the ranking).
- A model with a missing or unreadable value on a criterion is not ranked but listed; if that criterion is removed, the model comes back.
- The user can exclude models from the ranking; m and all ranks are recomputed with the remaining models. Because the relative rank is scaled by the best/worst values, a single extremely poor model pulls the others closer together (Poudel and Cao 2013 removed two methods for this reason).
- Criteria that give the same relative rank for all models (e.g. FI = 1 − SSE/SST and prediction R²; RMSE and MSE) are reported; with equal weights the same information is counted twice. The tool does not drop the criterion automatically.
- Repeated model names are distinguished by row number.
- AIC and BIC are comparable only between models fitted to the same data; the tool cannot check this and writes it as a note on screen.

**Radar chart:** The axes are the selected criteria, each model a polygon; the radius is 1 (best) at the centre and m (worst) on the outer circle. A small area is a good model (Poudel and Cao 2013, Figure 2). The user shows the best n, the worst n and also models selected by name. At least 3 criteria are required.

**Verification:** `tests/ref/make_rank_reference.py` generates the synthetic sample workbook (`assets/lab/rank/ornek/ornek-olcutler.xlsx`; a description row above the header, a repeated name, a missing value, R² = FI, an extremely poor model) and computes reference ranks with numpy in three scenarios (all models; extremely poor model excluded; the model with a missing value coming back when a criterion is removed). During development the per-criterion relative ranks in the user's sample Excel file were also reproduced exactly (with the same direction choices); this file was not put in the repository because it is research data.
- Poudel, K.P., Cao, Q.V. 2013. Evaluation of methods to predict Weibull parameters for characterizing diameter distributions. *Forest Science* 59(2): 243–252. **[B]** (Eq. 10 and Figure 2 checked in the PDF provided by the user)

---

## 10. Height–Diameter Modelling, fixed-effects version (Module 5, `assets/lab/hd/calc.js`, `assets/lab/core/nls.js`, `assets/lab/core/stats.js`)
**Input:** Tree table (CSV/TSV/paste/XLSX) or confirmed data package: diameter (cm or mm → cm), measured height (m, dm or cm → m; may be empty), optional plot, species (a single species can be selected) and filter column (e.g. period). Number reading rules as in §5.
**Breast height bh** is a user setting (default 1,30 m; 0 < bh < 3). All models are of the form h = bh + f(d); they are fitted to z = h − bh.
**Row statuses** (none is deleted, their counts are reported): diameter empty / unreadable / ≤ 0; height unreadable; **height ≤ bh** (cannot have a DBH: not included in the fit, the measured value stays unchanged in the export); **height empty** → tree to be predicted; diameter and height valid → fitting data.

### Models (d cm, h m; parameters are for these units)
| Id | Name | Form | p | Asymptote | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| naslund | Näslund | h = bh + d²/(a + b·d)² | 2 | bh + 1/b² (b > 0) | Näslund (1936) |
| michailoff | Michailoff | h = bh + a·exp(−b/d) | 2 | bh + a | Michailoff (1943) |
| curtis | Curtis | h = bh + a·(d/(1 + d))^b | 2 | bh + a | Curtis (1967) |
| power | Power | h = bh + a·d^b | 2 | none | Stoffels and van Soest (1953) |
| richards | Chapman–Richards | h = bh + a·(1 − exp(−b·d))^c | 3 | bh + a | Richards (1959) |
| weibull | Weibull | h = bh + a·(1 − exp(−b·d^c)) | 3 | bh + a | Yang, Kozak and Smith (1978) |

**Verification of the parameterisation:** The forms are identical to the `HDmodels` help page in Mehtätalo's `lmfor` R package (CRAN, search.r-project.org, read on 9 Nov 2026) **[B]**; the Chapman–Richards, Weibull and power forms were also compared with Zhang et al. (2014, Table 2) **[B]**. Note: the Curtis form is a·(d/(1+d))^b, not a·d/(1+d)^b. Michailoff's function appears also to be referred to as "Schumacher" in the Turkish literature (the table of Carus and Çatal 2017 could not be read as text) **[D]**; the tool uses the name from lmfor. Because the "Chapman" reference has not been verified, only Richards (1959) is given.
**Expected signs:** All parameters are expected to be > 0; otherwise the fit is given but a warning is written. For Näslund, additionally, a + b·d > 0 must hold for every d in the fitting data (otherwise the curve has a pole; the step is rejected). In Chapman–Richards and Weibull b > 0 is required.

### Fitting
- **Method:** least squares on the z scale, Levenberg–Marquardt (Marquardt's diagonal scaling, Nielsen's damping update), analytic derivatives (checked against central differences in the test).
- **Starting values** only from the fitted data themselves: Näslund d/√z = a + b·d (OLS); Michailoff ln z = ln a − b/d; Curtis ln z = ln a + b·ln(d/(1+d)); power ln z = ln a + b·ln d; Chapman–Richards on the grid a ∈ {1,05; 1,3; 1,8; 3}·max z and c ∈ {0,6; 1; 1,5; 2,5} b = Σd·(−ln(1 − (z/a)^(1/c)))/Σd²; Weibull with the same a values ln(−ln(1 − z/a)) = ln b + c·ln d (OLS). At most four starts with the smallest starting SSE are tried; it stops when two starts reach the same minimum (relative SSE difference ≤ 10⁻¹⁰); the converged solution with the smallest SSE is taken.
- **Convergence:** relative step ≤ 10⁻¹⁰; or, on an accepted step, relative SSE decrease ≤ 10⁻¹⁴ and relative step ≤ 10⁻⁶; or scaled gradient max_j |Jⱼᵀr| / √(‖Jⱼ‖²·SSE) ≤ 10⁻⁸ (the cosine criterion in MINPACK/SciPy). If all steps are rejected (damping exhausted), the point counts as converged only if the scaled gradient ≤ 10⁻⁶. At most 1000 iterations.
- **Statuses:** *converged*; *insufficient data* (n < p + 2 or number of distinct diameters < p + 1); *no starting value found*; *did not converge* (iteration limit or no step); *diverged*: any |θ| > 10⁸ or asymptote (excluding bh) > 50 × max(h − bh) — **a tool rule, not a literature value**; meaning "the asymptote cannot be determined with these data". The parameters of a non-converged model are not shown and it is not used for prediction.
- **Warning:** if the estimated correlation of two parameters is |r| > 0,99 (only when the covariance can be computed), a "hard to separate" note.

### Performance criteria (e = measured − predicted)
Bias ē, MAE, RMSE = √(SSE/n) (same definition as §7), residual standard error σ̂ = √(SSE/(n − p)). All models are fitted and compared **with the same fitting trees**.

### Inference: standard error, intervals, AIC (when are they given?)
- If a plot column is selected and the fitting data contain **≥ 2 plots**: trees on the same plot are not independent; the standard errors of the fixed-effects model come out smaller than they are. In this version **no standard error, parameter confidence interval, mean-curve confidence interval, individual-tree prediction interval or AIC/BIC is given** (mixed-effects models are in a later stage). Comparison is done by plot-level cross-validation.
- A single plot or no plot column: trees are assumed independent and this is stated on screen (if there is no column, with the warning "invalid if the data come from more than one plot").
- **Covariance:** V = σ̂²(JᵀJ)⁻¹, σ̂² = SSE/(n − p) (asymptotic; same as `nls`/`curve_fit`). Parameter 95% confidence interval θ̂ ± t(0,975; n − p)·SE.
- **95% confidence interval of the mean curve:** ŷ(d) ± t·√(gᵀVg), g = ∂f/∂θ (delta method). It is the uncertainty of the **mean** height at a given diameter.
- **95% prediction interval for an individual tree:** ŷ(d) ± t·√(σ̂² + gᵀVg). It is for the height of a single tree; it assumes independent, normally distributed errors with constant variance. For information, residual standard deviations in tertiles of the predicted height are shown (for a visual check of the assumption; no threshold).
- **AIC/BIC:** ln L = −n/2·[ln 2π + ln(SSE/n) + 1], k = p + 1 (including σ); AIC = 2k − 2 ln L, BIC = k ln n − 2 ln L (same as R's `logLik.nls` definition). Because all models are fitted to the same observations and the same response scale (h, m, untransformed), they are comparable; ΔAIC is relative to the smallest AIC.

### Cross-validation
- **Plot-level** (≥ 2 plots; default): with ≤ 20 plots each fold is one plot (leave-one-plot-out); with more, the plots are shuffled with seed 20261109 and distributed in turn over 10 folds; the trees of a plot are never split. Warning with fewer than 5 plots.
- **Tree-level:** trees are shuffled with the same seed and distributed over min(10, n) folds; the screen states that it does not measure generalisation to new plots. With a single plot, plot-level validation is **not offered** (tree-level is done and named as such).
- **No leakage:** in each fold the starting values and the fit use only the training trees; the held-out trees are only predicted. Preprocessing has no data-driven step (row rules are per row).
- **Same observations:** If any model fails to converge in a fold, that fold's trees are removed from the comparison **for all models** and the fold is listed.
- **Selection process:** in each fold the model with the smallest AIC on the training data (in the form n·ln(SSE/n) + 2k; only as a selection criterion) is selected and the held-out trees are predicted with it; the error of this row is the expected error of a selected model. The smallest CV error in its own row is optimistic.
- The number of held-out trees outside the training diameter range is reported.

### Prediction
- **Missing heights:** only for rows with empty height and valid diameter; with the single model chosen by the user (the tool does not select a model by itself). In a separate column (`boy_tahmin_m`); the measured height column (`boy_olculen_m`) and the source text (`boy_kaynak`) do not change; the `durum` column says whether the row is measured or predicted.
- **Out of range:** a prediction with d < min(d) or d > max(d) (fitting data) is marked "out of range"; the prediction is given but stays marked (`cap_araligi_disinda` in the export).
- **New diameters:** a list of diameters typed by the user (read with the decimal setting; unreadable and ≤ 0 values are listed).
- The predicted height is **not written** to the confirmed data package (`dataset:approved`) (in this round; the `height_source` field is later work).

### Numerical core and verification
| Function | Method | Verification |
|---|---|---|
| Regularised incomplete beta I_x(a, b) | Lentz continued fraction (Numerical Recipes `betacf`) | via the t distribution |
| Student t distribution function and percentile | with I_x; percentile by bisection (10⁻¹³) | SciPy `t.cdf`/`t.ppf`, < 10⁻⁹ |
| Seeded shuffle | mulberry32 + Fisher–Yates | Python counterpart, exact |
| Levenberg–Marquardt, covariance | above | SciPy `least_squares` |

`tests/ref/make_hd_reference.py` is independent of the JS code: `scipy.optimize.least_squares(method='lm')` with its own starting values (numerical Jacobian), covariance from a central-difference Jacobian, `scipy.stats.t`. Synthetic data are generated with numpy `default_rng(20261109)` (8 plots, plot-effect asymptote, 15% missing heights, two species, edge-case rows; **not real measurements**). References: on the full data the parameters, SSE, RMSE, MAE, bias, AIC/BIC of the six models; on plot P1 additionally SE, t percentile, mean-curve confidence and individual-tree prediction intervals at d = 8, 20, 35, 60 cm; plot-level and tree-level CV (same folds, selection within folds, pooled RMSE/MAE/bias, out-of-range count); single-species filter; t percentiles and distribution function; shuffle order. `tests/hd/test_hd.mjs` additionally tests the analytic derivatives, non-converging (linear, asymptote not determinable) data, insufficient data, the rejection of plot-level CV on a single plot, grouped folds with 30 plots, the removal of a failed fold from all models, that missing-height predictions go only to the missing rows, and the out-of-range marks. The browser test `tests/hd/e2e.mjs` checks that in the export the measured and predicted heights stay in separate columns.
- Näslund, M. 1936. Skogsförsöksanstaltens gallringsförsök i tallskog. *Meddelanden från Statens Skogsförsöksanstalt* 29: 1–169. **[İ]**
- Michailoff, I. 1943. Zahlenmässiges Verfahren für die Ausführung der Bestandeshöhenkurven. *Forstwissenschaftliches Centralblatt und Tharandter Forstliches Jahrbuch* 6: 273–279. **[İ]**
- Curtis, R.O. 1967. Height–diameter and height–diameter–age equations for second-growth Douglas-fir. *Forest Science* 13: 365–375. **[İ]**
- Stoffels, A., van Soest, J. 1953. The main problems in sample plots. *Nederlands Bosbouwtijdschrift* 25: 190–199. **[İ]**
- Richards, F.J. 1959. A flexible growth function for empirical use. *Journal of Experimental Botany* 10: 290–300. **[İ]**
- Yang, R.C., Kozak, A., Smith, J.H.G. 1978. The potential of Weibull-type functions as flexible growth curves. *Canadian Journal of Forest Research* 8: 424–431. **[İ]**
- Huang, S., Titus, S.J., Wiens, D.P. 1992. Comparison of nonlinear height–diameter functions for major Alberta tree species. *Canadian Journal of Forest Research* 22: 1297–1304. **[İ]**
- Mehtätalo, L., de-Miguel, S., Gregoire, T.G. 2015. Modeling height–diameter curves for prediction. *Canadian Journal of Forest Research* 45(7): 826–837. **[İ]** (from the reference list of the lmfor help page; there the title appears as "...for height prediction")
- Mehtätalo, L. `lmfor`: Functions for Forest Biometrics, R package, `HDmodels` help page. **[B]** (model forms)
- Zhang, X., Duan, A., Zhang, J., Xiang, C. 2014. Estimating tree height–diameter models with the Bayesian method. *The Scientific World Journal* 2014: 683691. **[B]** (Table 2: Chapman–Richards, Weibull, power forms)
- Reference list used as a secondary source: Mixed-effects modeling for tree height prediction models of Oriental beech in the Hyrcanian forests, *Journal of Forestry Research* (doi 10.1007/s11676-017-0551-z). The [İ] records above were verified in the reference list of this paper.
- Marquardt, D.W. 1963. An algorithm for least-squares estimation of nonlinear parameters. *J. SIAM* 11: 431–441. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026: eqs. 27–31, A scaled to correlation form, (A* + λI)δ* = g*, δⱼ = δⱼ*/√aⱼⱼ, i.e. (A + λ·diag A)δ = g as in `core/nls.js`; Marquardt's own λ strategy (×/÷ ν) is not used, the damping update is Nielsen's); Nielsen, H.B. 1999. Damping parameter in Marquardt's method. IMM-REP-1999-05, DTU. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026: eq. 1.10, μ₀ = τ·max (JᵀJ)ᵢᵢ, τ = 10⁻³ as in his Figure 2.1; eq. 2.5, if ρ > 0: μ := μ·max{1/γ, 1 − (β − 1)(2ρ − 1)^p}, ν := β, else μ := μν, ν := 2ν; the tool uses β = 2, γ = 3, p = 3, his recommended choice, on the Marquardt-scaled system); Press et al., *Numerical Recipes* (`betacf`). **[D]**

## 11. Inventory Sampling Planner, first version (Module 6, `assets/lab/sampling/calc.js`)
**Scope:** The approximate number of plots needed in simple random sampling to estimate the **mean** of a plot-level variable with a target **relative error**. This is not a power analysis for a hypothesis test. Stratified (allocation per stratum), systematic (variance), cluster and multistage sampling are not solved with this formula and the result is not presented as such (stated on screen and in the summary).

**Formulas (CV and E on the same percentage scale):**
- z = Φ⁻¹(1 − α/2), confidence level 80, 90, 95, 99% or another value between 50 and 100.
- n₀ = z²·(CV/E)².
- Finite frame correction **only if the user chooses that there is a finite, countable frame**: n = n₀ / [1 + (n₀ − 1)/M], M the number of units in the frame (integer ≥ 2). If not chosen, M is not used even if entered. The tool does not accept total forest area / plot size as M and offers no such helper.
- Reported numbers are rounded up (10⁻⁹ relative margin for floating-point noise: 35,0000000001 → 35). The unrounded n₀ and n are also shown.
- Existing sample: if the user declares that the existing plots are part of the same design (selected by simple random sampling from the same frame, same size and method), additional = max(0, n − n_existing); if not declared, no "additional" number is given. M < n_existing is rejected.
- Notes: n < 2 (at least 2 are needed for the SE of the mean); n₀ > 0,05·M (correction noticeable).

**Source of the CV:**
- *Pilot data:* each row is a plot value (e.g. m³/ha, m²/ha, trees/ha). Mean ȳ, s (denominator n − 1), CV = 100·s/ȳ. Empty, unreadable and **negative** values (plot-level totals cannot be negative) are reported with their row numbers and excluded; zero is a valid value (empty plot). If a plot id is selected, **a repeated id stops the computation** (tree-level rows or more than one period; a single period is selected with the filter). If the name of the value column resembles a tree-level measurement (dbh, çap, boy, h…), a warning is given. No computation is made until the user confirms that the rows are plot values and that the pilot was measured with the planned plot size and method. Tree-level diameter variability is not used in place of the CV of a plot-level variable.
- *Undefinedness:* n = 0 or 1 → no s; ȳ ≤ 0 → CV and relative error undefined (no result given); s = 0 → CV = 0 (no result given). If pilot n < 10, a "CV uncertain" warning (tool threshold, not a literature value).
- *Manual CV:* a positive number; its source is written to the summary as free text. Optional number of existing plots.
**Invalid inputs:** CV ≤ 0 or unreadable, E ∉ (0, 100), confidence level ∉ (50, 100), M missing/not an integer/< 2: the reasons are listed instead of a result.

**Assumptions (in the summary and on screen):** simple random and independent selection; the sampling distribution of the mean is approximately normal (z); small samples require t and the true number is somewhat larger; the CV is taken as known (if estimated from a pilot, it is uncertain); the CV is for the same variable, plot size and population; E is relative to the mean; no allowance is added for inaccessible/unmeasurable plots.

**Outputs:** total required number, (if declared) additional number, n₀, z and the computation written out with numbers; a table for E ∈ {2,5; 5; 7,5; 10; 12,5; 15; 20; 25; 30} and the user's E and an n–E chart; assumption summary (TXT), table (CSV), Excel (summary, table, pilot values).

**Acceptance example:** z = 1,96, CV = 30%, E = 10%, no correction: n₀ = 1,96²·9 = 34,5744 → **35**. With z(95%) = 1,959964, n₀ = 34,5731 → 35.

**Verification:** `tests/ref/make_sampling_reference.py` (independent of the JS): z with `scipy.stats.norm.ppf`; pilot mean, s (ddof = 1), CV with numpy; n₀, the correction and rounding up in plain Python; E grid (no M, M = 500, M = 60). The sample pilot file is generated with numpy `default_rng(20261109)` (14 plots, lognormal; **not real measurements**). `tests/sampling/test_sampling.mjs` additionally tests that n increases as E decreases, that the correction is applied only when selected and never increases n, invalid inputs, repeated ids, negative/unreadable rows, a zero mean and a constant pilot. Normal distribution: Φ(z) = ½[1 + P(½, z²/2)] (regularised incomplete gamma: series / Lentz continued fraction), Φ⁻¹ by bisection (10⁻¹⁴); < 10⁻¹² against SciPy.
- Israel, G.D. 1992 (revised 2009). *Determining Sample Size.* University of Florida IFAS Extension, PEOD6. doi:10.32473/EDIS-PD006-1992. **[B]** (Eq. 3: n = n₀/[1 + (n₀ − 1)/N]; Eq. 7: n₀ = Z²σ²/e² for the mean; based on Cochran)
- Cochran, W.G. 1963. *Sampling Techniques*, 2nd edition. Wiley. **[İ]** (from Israel's reference list); 3rd edition (1977) **[B]** for the passages listed in §6 (the sample-size formulas of this section were not checked in it).
- FAO. *Assessing the status of logged-over production forests: the development of a rapid appraisal technique* (fao.org/4/ac838e). **[B]** (n = 4·CV²/AE%², z² ≈ 4 for 95%; forestry use of the relative CV/E form)
- Relative form: writing σ = CV·ȳ/100 and e = E·ȳ/100 in n₀ = Z²σ²/e² gives n₀ = z²(CV/E)² (algebraic, requires no source).

---

## 12. Diameter distribution engine (revision, `assets/lab/dist/`; specification `docs/DIST_SPEC.md`)
Computational core of the multi-distribution tool. Live since 10 Nov 2026 (`diameter-distribution.php`, 2.0.0-beta.1; 2.0.0-beta.2 with P10, 2.0.0-beta.3 with P06; user decisions); the 1.0.0 Weibull tool of §8 was moved to `legacy/weibull/` and its formulas continue unchanged in this engine (P05). This section grows as the stages are completed.

### P01: registry, result contract, data contract (10 Nov 2026)
- **Concepts are kept separate:** distribution family; method (kind: direct estimation from data `fit`, recovery from summaries `recover`, estimation from stand variables `stand`; estimation principle: maximum likelihood, moments…); numerical solver (written in the result, not an option). Only registered (implemented and tested) families and methods appear in the interface.
- **Family interface:** parameter schema (name, role, unit, bounds), support, pdf/logpdf, cdf/logcdf, sf/logsf, quantile, mean, variance. Logarithms are computed directly (the logarithm of a rounded probability is not taken). For Weibull, ln F = ln(1 − e^(−u)), u = ((d − a)/b)^c: for u < 10⁻⁵ ln u − u/2 + u²/24, for u < ln 2 ln(−expm1(−u)), above that log1p(−e^(−u)).
- **Structured errors:** observation outside the support (`support.outside`: count, example, bound), invalid parameter (`param.invalid`), undefined moment (`moment.undefined`). An error becomes a result record without parameters; no small constant is added to the density.
- **Result contract `dist-result/1`:** family, method, kind, estimation principle, status (`ok`, `boundary`, `noconverge`, `invalid`, `error`), data (plot, period, species, n, unit), parameters and their units, fixed parameters (value, origin: zero/user/data), free parameters, objective value, ln L (on the original diameter scale), convergence (solver, iterations, tolerance, interval), warnings, issues, source id. Fields that do not apply stay empty.
- **Data contract:** the unit of analysis is plot × period (if a period column is selected); repeated measurements of the same plot are not merged. In pooled mode, for each period all trees are combined with equal weight (not per-hectare weighted; this is stated on screen). d in cm; n is the number of observed trees. Unreadable diameters and diameters not greater than zero are counted with their source row and do not enter the analysis.
