- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_taper_sv02a2.py` (equation written separately from the paper; volume `scipy.integrate.quad` epsrel 1e−12; height by a 20 000-interval scan + `brentq`; random coefficient sets (±25% of an arbitrary nominal set; belonging to no species) and an artificial set with a negative tip exponent). `tests/taper/test_sv02a2.mjs` 523/523: diameters 1e−12, 105 volumes against scipy (worst relative 1.1e−13; acceptance 1e−10), heights 1e−9 m, d(h(d)) = d (worst 1.4e−11), the d(1.3) identity, tip behaviour.

### SV-02/a3: taper equation fitting (16 Nov 2026; TOOLS_SPEC TP02)
`assets/lab/taper/fit.js` (DOM-free). Data: stem measurement table (`core/stems.js`, `stem-sections/1`; the same import as the Sectional Volume tool); the bark side is selected (ob: outside-bark diameters, ib: inside-bark), D (DBH outside bark) and H from the tree. Faulty trees, and points without the selected diameter or above the height, are excluded and counted; in kozak02 points at the tip (h ≥ H; X = 0, ln X undefined) are excluded.
- **Scale (written in the result):** `d` (default): unweighted nonlinear least squares on d (cm) (Levenberg–Marquardt, `core/nls.js`), in both forms. `source`: the source's scale: mb76 → NLS on y = d²/D² (Max and Burkhart 1976 fit y); kozak02 → ordinary least squares on ln d (on this scale the model is linear in ln a₀, a₁, a₂, b₁…b₆; Kozak 2004 estimates with this linearisation; no back-transformation correction is applied, note `no-backtransform-correction` in the result). The two scales give different coefficients; RMSE, bias and classes are always on the d scale (cm).
- **Starting values:** mb76: join point grid (a₁ 0.50…0.95 step 0.05, a₂ 0.02…0.30 step 0.02, a₂ < a₁), for each pair the b's by linear OLS on y, the pair with the smallest residual sum of squares; kozak02: log-linear OLS. Tool rule (not a literature value).
- **Standard errors:** σ̂²(JᵀJ)⁻¹, assuming independent observations; the points of the same tree are not independent, the result carries the label "independent-observations" (no mixed-effects fitting at this stage). For kozak02 on the source scale no SE is given.
- **Output:** coefficients, status, iterations, n (points), number of trees, excluded items, RMSE and bias (observed − predicted, cm), n, bias and RMSE by relative-height classes (10 classes), per-point residuals; per tree, the **volume from the Sectional Volume tool** (its settings; default Smalian, ends, cone tip) and the profile integral over the same height interval (from the ground if the section volume includes the stump, otherwise from the cutting height to the tip), the difference and relative difference. A successful fit becomes a coefficient set with `origin: 'fitted'` through `fitToSet` (fit summary `fit`; the source field is still required).
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_taper_sv02a3.py` (models written separately; `scipy.optimize.least_squares` 'lm', tolerances 1e−15, its own finite-difference Jacobian and its own start (true values × 1.05); `numpy.linalg.lstsq` for Kozak ln d; simulation: 30 trees, D 12–50 cm, H 10–30 m, points at 0.15 m, 1.3 m and every 1 m; true coefficients are test values; noise-free and with N(0, 0.4 cm) noise). `tests/taper/test_sv02a3.mjs` 42/42: coefficients against SciPy (worst relative 1.7e−7; acceptance 1e−6), the true coefficients are recovered on noise-free data (worst 4.4e−13), SSE, SE and its label, the classes cover all points, RMSE, volume comparison (largest relative difference in the simulation 3.4%), the fitted set is valid and requires a source, a faulty tree is excluded.

### Page: Taper Models (16 Nov 2026; TOOLS_SPEC TP04; 1.0.0-beta.1)
`stem-taper.php`, `assets/lab/taper/ui.js`, `i18n.js`. Id SV-02.
- **Model:** coefficient library (`core/coef-ui.js`, `tool: 'taper'`); the set's bark side is a required tool field (`meta.bark`; the core reads `output.bark` or `meta.bark`). One outside-bark and one inside-bark set can be selected; if both are selected, the results and the chart give both. Or fitting from stem measurements (SV-02/a3; the stem table and sample data of the Sectional Volume tool; units m, cm, mm); the fitted set is saved to the library with its source (`origin: 'fitted'`). Parameters whose standard error is larger than the estimate are listed with a warning (the data do not determine that parameter; e.g. butt swell when there are few measurements near the base).
- **Trees:** a single tree (D, H) or a tree table (plot, tree, D, H). Settings (tool rule): cutting height (default 0.3 m), optional top diameter, profile table step (default 1 m).
- **Merchantable volume** (`merchantable`, calc.js): total volume from the cut to the tip; if a top diameter is given, the highest root of d(h) = d_top above the cut (where the diameter last falls to that value; with multiple roots the uppermost, with a note) and the volume from the cut to there; if the diameter at the cut is smaller than the top diameter, merchantable volume is 0 (note). Verification `test_tp01.mjs` (in the Python reference the uppermost root with brentq and the quad volume; 21 cases, 1e−10).
- **Results:** tree × set table (total, top-diameter height, merchantable volume, d(1.3), notes: validity range, undefined region, tip); profile chart of the selected tree (301-point grid; outside bark solid, inside bark dashed line) and profile table; Excel (Info: tool, version, date, language, settings, sets and their sources; Trees; Profile), CSV, JSON.
- **Verification:** `tests/taper/e2e.mjs` 21/21 (in source and bundled mode): fitting with sample data (decimal comma detection, column guessing, coefficients against the engine in Node 1e−6, excluded tree, class and volume tables, weak parameter warning), rejection of a record without a source, single-tree total/merchantable volume and profile table the same as the engine, Excel sheets and CSV, pasted tree table (with an invalid-tree note), session restore, English, phone and dark theme, no page errors.

### SV-03: log length limit (17 Nov 2026; `logCount`, `logLimit`; Taper Models 1.0.0-beta.3)
Source **[B]**: Kershaw, J.A., Ducey, M.J., Beers, T.W., Husch, B. 2016. *Forest Mensuration*, 5th edition. Wiley (pp. 160–200 set aside by the user, 17 Nov 2026):
- §6.4.1 (pp. 168–169), worked example, step 3: "Subtracting 1 ft for the stump and allowing 0.3 ft for trim, then number of 16-ft logs are determined: L = ((72 − 17.3)/16.3) + 1 = 3.3 + 1 = 4 logs". That is, the length from the cut to the top limit is divided by log length + trim allowance, and only whole logs are counted.
- §7.4.1 (p. 195): trim allowance ("trim allowance"), the extra length cut with each log, 3–6 in.
- §7.4.3 (p. 198): a usable log has a minimum length (e.g. a log shorter than 8 ft is cull). Note to Table 6.14: top pieces shorter than the minimum length may be measured as fractions (in the tool, the option "count the remaining short piece").
- §5.3 (top diameter limit) was not read separately; the top diameter limit already existed on the SV-02 page (the height at which the profile last falls to the top diameter).

Implementation: n = ⌊(h_top − h_cut)/(L + t)⌋ (the same integer part as the book's form ((h_top − h_cut − (L + t))/(L + t)) + 1; exact multiples are counted as whole with a 1e−9 relative tolerance). h_top is the height of the top diameter, or the tree's tip if no top diameter is entered. The i-th log runs from b_i = h_cut + i(L + t) to b_i + L, the trim allowance from b_i + L to b_i + L + t. Remainder r = h_top − h_cut − n(L + t): "not counted" (default; the book's example) or "counted" (if r − t > 0 and, when a minimum log length is entered, r − t ≥ minimum); if L < minimum, no log is produced. Volumes are the integral of the selected profile set (§17); logs + short piece + trim allowances + remainder = merchantable volume from the cut to h_top. The values in the book (16 ft, 0.3 ft, 1 ft, 8 ft) are only an example; the tool has no defaults, lengths are metric and come from the user (K1). Defect deduction (§7.4.1) and scaling rules (board-foot, SV-05) are out of scope.
Test: `node tests/taper/test_sv03.mjs` (8; the book's example 4 logs; `tests/ref/make_taper_sv03.py` standard library only, log count with the book's form and Simpson volumes on a Max–Burkhart profile, 42 trees; balance), browser `tests/taper/e2e.mjs` (39).

**R code (21 Nov 2026):** the fit (SV-02/a3) can be downloaded as an R script (`assets/lab/taper/rcode.js`) with the observations the tool used as CSV (the stem table itself is read and checked by the tool): `nls` on d or on y = d²/D² (mb76), `nls` on d or `lm` on ln d (kozak02), from the tool's starting values (fallbacks as for Site Index); measures on the d scale. Checked in R by `scripts/rcode-check.sh` (`tests/rcode/taper.mjs`: both forms × both scales on the noisy simulated stems; parameters, standard errors, n, trees, RMSE, bias agree with the tool).

## 18. Growth function fitter (`assets/lab/growth/calc.js`; PLATFORM_PLAN SQ-03)

### SQ-03/a1: forms, fitting and derived quantities (16 Nov 2026)
A growth curve is fitted to the user's own age–size data (height, diameter, volume…; the variable and its unit are the user's). There are no built-in coefficients (K1).

| Id | Form | Source status |
|---|---|---|
| `cr` | Chapman–Richards y = b₀(1 − e^(−b₁t))^b₂ | **[B]** Hernández-Cuevas et al. 2018, Cuadro 2 (§15); originals Richards 1959, Chapman 1961 [İ] |
| `schu` | Schumacher y = b₀·e^(−b₁/t) | **[B]** Hernández-Cuevas et al. 2018, Cuadro 2 (written in the source as H = b₀·exp(−b₁/E)) |
| `gomp` | Gompertz y = b₀·exp(−b₁e^(−b₂t)) | **[A]** Fekedulegn, D., Mac Siurtain, M.P., Colbert, J.J. 1999. Parameter estimation of nonlinear growth models in forestry. *Silva Fennica* 33(4): 327–336, Table 1 (citing: Draper and Smith 1981) + worked example (Tables 9 and 11). Discrepancy: the `growthmodels` 1.3.1 documentation writes exp(−k^t) (most likely a typesetting error); the tool uses Fekedulegn's exp(−kt) form, and the published coefficients reproduce the estimates in Table 11 exactly with this form |
| `logi` | Logistic y = b₀/(1 + b₁e^(−b₂t)) | **[A]** Fekedulegn et al. 1999 Table 1 + `growthmodels` documentation (same) + worked example |
| `mono` | Monomolecular y = b₀(1 − b₁e^(−b₂t)) | **[A]** Fekedulegn et al. 1999 + `growthmodels` (same) |
| `weib` | Weibull y = b₀ − b₁e^(−b₂t^b₃) | **[A]** Fekedulegn et al. 1999 + `growthmodels` (same) + worked example |

- **Korf not added:** Zeide, B. 1993. Analysis of growth equations. *Forest Science* 39(3): 594–616 was read (16 Nov 2026): Table 1 gives Korf as y = a·e^(−b·t^(−c)), y′ = abc·t^(−c−1)·e^(−b·t^(−c)) (original Korf 1939, cited); it counts Schumacher as the special case c = 1 (Terazaki 1915, Johnson 1935, Schumacher 1939). The other five forms are in the same parameterisation as Zeide's Table 1 (Weibull as the three-parameter a(1 − e^(−bt^c)); our four-parameter form reduces to it at b₁ = b₀). For [A] there is no second independent secondary source or numerical verification (Zeide has no worked example with Korf coefficients); it is not added until a source arrives. Fekedulegn's four-parameter Chapman–Richards (with β) and the von Bertalanffy/Richards forms were not added (overparameterised; the paper's own finding is also meaningless parameters).
- **Parameter region:** all parameters > 0 (increasing curves with an asymptote); the fit stays in this region.
- **Fitting:** Levenberg–Marquardt (`core/nls.js`), analytic gradients, stopping xtol = ftol = 10⁻¹⁵, gtol 10⁻¹²; start: the best five points of a small grid (asymptote 1.05–4 × largest y, rate 0.2–4/largest t; shape values by form; tool rule) by residual sum of squares, the convergence with the smallest SSE. Criteria: SSE, RMSE = √(SSE/n), bias (observed − predicted), σ̂ = √(SSE/(n − p)) (Fekedulegn's RSE), AIC = 2(p + 1) − 2 ln L (normal likelihood, σ ML; the definition in the Height–Diameter tool). SE σ̂²(JᵀJ)⁻¹, labelled **assuming independent observations** (the measurements of the same series are not independent).
- **Derived quantities (definition):** current annual increment CAI = dy/dt (analytic), mean annual increment MAI = y/t; age at the inflection point (maximum of CAI; closed form: CR ln(b₂)/b₁ (b₂ > 1), Schumacher b₁/2, Gompertz and logistic ln(b₁)/b₂ (b₁ > 1), Weibull ((b₃ − 1)/(b₂b₃))^(1/b₃) (b₃ > 1), monomolecular none); age of peak MAI: the first point in the interval (0, 10·largest age] where t·y′(t) − y(t) crosses **from positive to negative** (maximum of MAI; 4000-interval scan + Brent; a crossing from negative to positive is the minimum of MAI and is not counted as a peak; otherwise "none"). In the first version the first crossing in either direction was taken, and in Gompertz with y(0) > 0 the minimum of MAI was written as the peak; it was noticed on screen, the test had not caught it because the Python reference was written with the same definition; both were corrected.
- **Findings from Fekedulegn's worked example** (Table 11 data, Norway spruce top height; in the test only, CC BY-SA 4.0): the published coefficients for Gompertz, logistic and Weibull reproduce the estimates in Table 11 within |Δ| ≤ 0.006 m (rounding to two decimals); the monomolecular column deviates systematically by up to 0.011 m (the table probably comes from another iteration). The paper's Gompertz coefficients did not reach the least-squares optimum: SSE 0.12569, optimum (this tool and SciPy) 0.12431 (b₀ = 25.4847, b₁ = 2.5951, b₂ = 0.036777; the paper's Richards column at m → 0 is also close to these values). Monomolecular, logistic and Weibull agree with the published values within 2e−5.
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_growth_sq03.py` (models written separately; `scipy.optimize.least_squares` 'lm', its own start and finite-difference Jacobian; derived ages by `mpmath` 40-digit numerical derivative + scan + `findroot`, without using the closed forms; Fekedulegn's data and a simulation for each form (40 points, N(0, 0.4) noise)). `tests/growth/test_sq03.mjs` 64/64: Table 11 formula check, coefficients and SSE against SciPy (1e−6 / 1e−9), inflection and MAI peak ages (1e−7), CAI = dy/dt, MAI = y/t, SE label and σ̂, few observations, fitted set, reading issues with their rows.

### SQ-03 page: Growth Function Fitter (16 Nov 2026; `growth-functions.php`, 1.0.0-beta.1)
`assets/lab/growth/ui.js`, `i18n.js`. Steps: Data (table or paste; series optional, age and size required; variable name and unit; decimal setting; unreadable rows with their numbers; simulated sample `ornek/ornek-buyume.csv`, `tests/ref/make_growth_sample.py`), Models (forms and source statuses; why Korf is absent), Results (comparison table: coefficients and SE, RMSE, bias, σ̂, AIC (the smallest ★ and shown first in the detail), asymptote, inflection age, MAI peak age; observations and all curves; CAI–MAI chart for the selected model (y axis based on ages after the smallest observed age, since MAI grows at t → 0), residuals, curve table (with adjustable step); save to the coefficient library with a source (`tool: 'growth'`); Excel: Info, Comparison, Predictions, Curves; CSV; JSON). The session's pending save is written when the page closes (`pagehide`) (the same fix also applied to the Taper Models page).
- **Verification:** `tests/growth/e2e.mjs` 24/24 (in source and bundled mode): reading sample data, six forms, RMSE, AIC, asymptote, inflection and MAI peak ages on screen the same as the engine, smallest-AIC mark and selection, charts, curve table (y, CAI, MAI), rejection of a record without a source, saving to the library, Excel sheets and full-precision predictions, single form, session, English, phone, no page errors. `test_sq03.mjs` 74/74 (checks that the MAI peak is a local maximum were added).

## 19. Bucking (`assets/lab/taper/bucking.js`; TOOLS_SPEC §6.2, TP03; PLATFORM_PLAN SV-06)

### SV-06/a1: priority order and dynamic programming (16 Nov 2026)
With a taper coefficient set (§17), the merchantable stem of a tree is bucked into the user's products. There are no built-in products, lengths, diameter limits or prices (K1); the product list is saved and loaded as `bucking-products/1` JSON.

- **Sources:**
  - **[B]** Grondin, F. 1998. Improvements of the dynamic programming algorithm for tree bucking. *Wood and Fiber Science* 30(1): 91–104. Recursion (3): stem_value(0, j) = max_{i ∈ I(j)} {stem_value(0, j − i) + log_value(j − i, j)}; positions in steps of p from the butt, predefined lengths are divisible by p, the piece left at the stem end is valued separately. Grondin's log_value comes from a sawing simulation; here it is replaced by the user's value per m³ × piece volume.
  - **[B]** Bennemann, C., Lussier, J.-M., Labelle, E.R. 2025. An open-source tree bucking optimizer based on dynamic programming. *Forests* 16: 780 (BuckR). The same staged recursion (value of the added log + best value of the previous cumulative length; Dykstra 1984 eq. 10.6); product = length (including over-length) + small-end diameter limits + value per m³; a zero-value log can be produced but is not selected. In Table 3 of the paper the length of product no. 4 is written as "45" (should be 450); the numerical result of the verification example (Dykstra) is not in the paper.
  - **[D]** Pnevmaticos, S.M., Mann, S.H. 1972. Dynamic programming in tree bucking. *Forest Products Journal* 22(2): 26–30: the first dynamic programming bucking model, not read (original not obtainable (22 Nov 2026, user decision; see the legend)); cited through Grondin and Bennemann et al. Grondin writes that his own form differs from it; the one implemented is Grondin's.
- **Product:** name, minimum small-end diameter dMin (cm; the bark side of the product list must be the same as that of the coefficient set: there is no bark model, no conversion), length L (fixed) or L…Lmax step Lstep, trim allowance (cm), priority (1 highest), value per m³ (optional; the currency is the user's).
- **Tool rules (not from the source, written in the result):**
  - Grid: upward from the cut (stump height, default 0.3 m, the same as on the page) in steps of Δ (default 0.1 m). Length + trim allowance must be an exact multiple of Δ; otherwise the product is rejected (Grondin: lengths are divisible by p).
  - A piece occupies length + trim allowance; the small-end diameter is the profile diameter at the upper end of the piece; its volume and value cover the whole piece (Bennemann et al.: length including over-length, m³ value × volume).
  - Merchantable stem [cut, hTop]; hTop is the highest root of the merchantable top diameter (§17 `merchantable`; H if there is no top diameter). If the diameter at the cut is smaller than the top diameter, there is no merchantable stem.
  - A one-step waste can be left anywhere (option f(j) = f(j − 1)); the recursion covers all cutting sequences even if the profile does not decrease monotonically with height.
  - **Priority method** (TOOLS_SPEC §6.2 (1)): upward from the butt, at each position the highest-priority product that fits (longest of its lengths first); stops when none fits.
  - **Value method** (dynamic programming): maximises Σ value (Σ volume if the objective "volume" is selected). On ties the earlier choice stays (waste first, then priority order, from long to short length). With the value objective every product must have a value.
- **Volume balance:** piece volumes are the profile integral (§17 `volume`); stump V(0, cut) + pieces + waste (non-piece part of the merchantable stem) + top V(hTop, H) = total stem volume V(0, H).
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_bucking_sv06.py` (profiles written separately in Python, volumes `scipy.integrate.quad`, top height by scan + `brentq`; on a 0.5 m grid, for trees with a merchantable stem ≤ 8 m, **all cutting sequences** were enumerated, 23–2140 sequences; the recursion is not shared; the priority method was replayed with its rule; Max and Burkhart Table 3 and arbitrary Kozak coefficients, arbitrary products and values: test values only). `tests/taper/test_sv06.mjs` 884/884: in 36 cases the best value and best volume of dynamic programming = brute force (1e−9), the pieces of the priority method = reference, total and stump volume = quad; in each method pieces + waste = total (1e−10), small-end diameter ≥ dMin, pieces do not overlap; in 8 of the 36 cases dynamic programming gives a higher value than the priority method. Also large trees on a 0.1 m grid (Max and Burkhart, Kozak), tree table totals, bark side mismatch, off-grid length, missing value, JSON round trip.

### SV-06 page: product bucking in Taper Models (16 Nov 2026; `stem-taper.php`, 1.0.0-beta.2)
No separate page was opened: the coefficient set and trees are already on the Taper Models page (user approval). In step 2 an optional "Bucking" card: diameter side of the products, bucking step (default 0.1 m), currency (label only), method (highest value: value or volume; priority order), editable product table (add/delete; download and load JSON, `bucking-products/1`); cutting height and top diameter from the volume settings. Errors (off-grid length, missing value, no profile set on that side) disable the Calculate button and are written with the product number. In step 3: method and settings, product summary (all trees; colour + pattern swatch), volume balance, per-tree table, stem drawing of the selected tree (profile silhouette; each product with colour and pattern, cuts as dashed lines, number label; SVG downloadable) and piece table; Products and Pieces sheets in Excel, pieces CSV, `bucking` in JSON. Because the bucking calculation changed, the tool version is 1.0.0-beta.2 (K4).
- **Verification:** `tests/taper/e2e.mjs` 33/33 (in source and bundled mode): calculation disabled without products and with an off-grid length, in the value method the product summary, total value, piece heights and small ends the same as the engine (`buckTree`), one drawing and one row per piece, a pattern per product, Excel sheets at full precision, the bucking changes when the minimum diameter and the method change and is the same as the engine, product list JSON round trip; guide section (`test_guide` 162/162).


## 20. Reproducibility layer (`assets/lab/core/record.js`; PLATFORM_PLAN DI-07)
No scientific calculation: this section defines the fingerprint and seal of the common run record added to the tools' JSON downloads (`run-record/1`, PROJECT.md §4c).

| Quantity | Definition |
|---|---|
| File fingerprint | SHA-256(the file's bytes as read); encoding, separator, header row and worksheet are written separately |
| Pasted table | SHA-256(UTF-8 bytes of the text) |
| Data package / JSON value | SHA-256(RFC 8785 canonical JSON): no whitespace, object keys in UTF-16 code unit order, numbers in the ECMAScript shortest representation, strings with `JSON.stringify` escapes |
| Record seal (`integrity.value`) | SHA-256(canonical JSON(record − `integrity`)); the record is first normalised as written to the file (NaN, ±∞ → null; undefined fields are dropped) |
| Build id (`build.commit`) | `deploy-commit.txt` on the server (written by the upload job after each release; not publicly accessible); null in the development environment |

Notes:
- The seal is not a signature: anyone can recompute it; it reveals accidental or deliberate changes, it does not prevent forgery. Independent check: produce the canonical text with any library implementing RFC 8785 and take its SHA-256. Python's `json.dumps(sort_keys=True)` output may differ in number representation (e.g. `1e-07` / `1e-7`) and key order (Unicode code point / UTF-16); it does not substitute for the canonical form.
- A one-byte difference (e.g. a CSV opened in Excel and saved again) changes the fingerprint; the verifier shows this as "no match". A data package downloaded from the Data Checker matches by its canonical form, independently of its formatting and of a UTF-8 BOM.
- Embedded data (optional): file bytes as base64, pasted text as UTF-8, data package as JSON; the verifier additionally checks that the embedded data agree with the fingerprint.

Sources:
- Rundgren, A., Jordan, B., Erdtman, S. 2020. *JSON Canonicalization Scheme (JCS).* RFC 8785, IETF. **[B]** (read on 22 Nov 2026: §3.2.1 no whitespace, §3.2.2.2 string escapes as ECMAScript, §3.2.2.3 numbers per ECMA-262 §7.1.12.1, §3.2.3 property names sorted by UTF-16 code units; standard; the implementation is tested with the RFC's §3.2.3 sorting and §3.2.4 sample input/output)
- NIST 2015. *Secure Hash Standard (SHS).* FIPS PUB 180-4. **[B]** (read on 22 Nov 2026: August 2015, SHA-256 in §6.2; the test vectors are on NIST's examples page, not in the standard; standard; SHA-256 is the browser's Web Crypto function, not implemented; test: Node `node:crypto` (OpenSSL) and the "abc", "" test vectors)

Test: `node tests/record/test_di07.mjs` (34), browser `node tests/record/e2e.mjs` (29).

## 21. Data Visualization (`assets/lab/dashboard/calc.js`; PLATFORM_PLAN DI-04)
No new scientific calculation. The panels use tested engines: plot × period N/ha and G/ha from the Plot and Stand engine in §6 (`computeAll`; combinations without records are "not measured" or "empty" by user choice); the density of the diameter distribution results in the records from the Diameter Distribution engine (in a truncated fit the conditional density f(x)/[F(U) − F(L)], DIST_SPEC P11); height curves from the Height–Diameter engine (h = bh + f(θ, d)). Defined in the dashboard:

| Quantity | Definition |
|---|---|
| Histogram | [lower, upper) classes, the first class starts at a multiple of the width, the largest value is in the last class; bar height n_k / (n · w) (density; total area 1) |
| Automatic class width | the 1, 2, 5 × 10^k closest to range/12 (rounded up) |
| Species share | ratio of the sums of tree count and basal area g = π/4 · (d/100)² (filtered records) |
| Mean N, G | unweighted arithmetic mean of the displayed plot × period values (not a design-based estimate) |
| Species filter | N/ha and G/ha the share of that species in the plot; 0 in a measured unit where the species does not occur |

Test: `node tests/dashboard/test_di04.mjs` (24; hand-counted classes and N, G; closed-form Weibull density and its truncated version; written Näslund curve), browser `node tests/dashboard/e2e.mjs` (22).

## 22. Biomass and carbon (`assets/lab/biomass/calc.js`; TOOLS_SPEC §5, BC01; PLATFORM_PLAN BC-01, BC-03, BC-04)
The site contains no species coefficients, wood densities, expansion factors, root ratios or carbon fractions (K1, K9): equation coefficients come from `model-coef/1` sets (source required), factors from user input.

**A. Allometric equations (tree level).** D cm (outside bark), H m, B kg dry matter. Registered forms (`biomass.<id>`):

| Id | Form | Scale |
|---|---|---|
| `pow.d` | B = a·D^b | linear |
| `pow.dh` | B = a·D^b·H^c | linear |
| `pow.d2h` | B = a·(D²H)^b | linear |
| `lin.d2h` | B = a + b·D²H | linear |
| `log.d` | ln B = a + b·ln D | ln |
| `log.dh` | ln B = a + b·ln D + c·ln H | ln |
| `log10.d` | log₁₀ B = a + b·log₁₀ D | log₁₀ |
| `log10.dh` | log₁₀ B = a + b·log₁₀ D + c·log₁₀ H | log₁₀ |

Source status: the forms are general function definitions applied with the user's coefficients; **definition exception [D]** (16 Nov 2026, user decision; of the same kind as the Huber, Smalian and Hart–Becking exception). Each result carries the source of the coefficient set used. log₁₀ forms are computed on their own scale (10^(…)), not silently converted to ln; the units are the form's units (D cm, H m, B kg), a set entered in other units is rejected, not converted.

Back-transformation correction (log forms): none; Snowdon ratio or user factor, multiplied by the value in the source (no formula); **Baskerville factor** CF = exp(SEE²/2), SEE the residual standard error of the regression on the natural logarithm scale (RSE; from the source), B = exp(a + …)·CF. Source status **[A]** (16 Nov 2026): Baskerville (1972), cited in: Chave et al. (2014) and Baia et al. (2025); two independent, peer-reviewed, open-access secondary sources, both read from the text: Chave et al. 2014, pp. 3180–3181, Equation 2: "As first shown by Baskerville (1972) … exp[ε] = exp(σ²/2) … AGBest = exp[σ²/2 + a + b ln(ρD²H)]", σ = √(Σεᵢ²/(N − p)), "residual standard error or RSE"; Baia et al. 2025, Methods: "a correction factor [CF = exp(RSE²/2)] based on the residual standard error (RSE) of the regression which was multiplied by the estimated AGB (Baskerville, 1972)" (ln scale). No discrepancy. Chave et al. note more refined corrections (Shen and Zhu 2008; Clifford et al. 2013); the tool offers only the Baskerville form. **For equations written with log₁₀** (since 1.0.0-beta.6, 22 Nov 2026, user decision) the SEE on the log₁₀ scale is first converted to the ln scale, SEE·ln 10 (ln 10 = 2.303), then CF = exp[(SEE·ln 10)²/2] and B = 10^(a + …)·CF. Source: Sprugel (1983) **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026, pp. 209–210). Before beta.6 this case gave `unsupported` ("source pending"). Other forms and coefficients of these sources (Chave et al. 2014 Model 1, the Baia et al. 2025 equations) are not embedded (K1).

Statuses: `ok`; `noheight` (no height for a form that needs H: not computed; height prediction is done in the Height–Diameter tool, those computed with predicted height are counted with `height_source`); `invalid` (invalid set, unit mismatch, D or H invalid, result not positive and finite: e.g. if `lin.d2h` gives a negative mass for a small tree, no number is shown); `unsupported` (a correction awaiting its source; since beta.6 no such case remains, the status is kept for the record format). A tree outside the validity range is computed and receives a warning; the numbers are reported.

Components: each component is a separate set; the sum of components is given only when all are computed. If a separate "total" equation is also entered, the difference (total − sum of components) and the relative difference are written: additivity is not assumed.

**B. Conversion from volume.** IPCC (2006), Volume 4, Chapter 2, Equation 2.8b **[B]** (read from the original text on 16 Nov 2026): C = Σ A·V·BCEF_S·(1 + R)·CF, BCEF_S = BEF_S·D. In the tool, at tree or unit level: AGB = V·D·BEF (or V·BCEF), BGB = AGB·R, total = AGB·(1 + R). The definitions are as in IPCC: V merchantable growing stock volume (m³), D basic wood density (Mg dry matter / m³), BEF expansion factor (–), BCEF conversion and expansion factor (Mg / m³), R ratio of below-ground to above-ground biomass (the expression "root/shoot ratio" in TOOLS_SPEC was corrected to the IPCC definition). If the bark side of the volume does not match the side for which the factors are defined, nothing is computed; that the volume definition (merchantable stem, total stem) is the same as the definition of the factors is the user's responsibility and is written in the result (BC02).

**Carbon.** C = B·CF, CF from the user per component, 0 < CF ≤ 1. CO₂ = C·44/12: ratio of molecular weights (IPCC 2006, Volume 4, Chapter 2, §2.2.3 **[B]**); a constant, not a coefficient (K9).

**Stand level.** Per plot × period Σ kg · 10000 / A / 1000 = Mg/ha (fixed-area plots; units via the `buildUnits` function of the Plot and Stand engine, §6). If a unit has a tree that cannot be computed, no unit total is given (a partial total is not shown as a total); their counts are written.

**BC02, page (17 Nov 2026; `biomass-carbon.php`, `computeAllometric`, `computeVolume`).** No new formula; the tree-level calculation of §22 is applied to a data package. The species mapping is explicit: if no set is selected for a component of a species, those trees are not computed for that component (`unmapped`, with row numbers), and another species' equation is not used; the choice "apply to all species" is made explicitly by the user. Tree carbon Σ component B·CF_component; the tree's total and carbon are given only when all its components are computed. Unit totals (Mg/ha) with the grouping in §6; no total in a unit with missing trees. In the volume path, the tree volume is read from a column of the table (when importing from a table, from the raw table with the row number; in confirmed data, from the `extra` field); a tree whose volume cannot be read is `novolume`. Test: `node tests/biomass/test_bc02.mjs` (12; reference `tests/ref/make_biomass_bc02.py`, with separate tree-by-tree and unit-by-unit loops; unmapped species, missing height and missing volume cases), browser `node tests/biomass/e2e.mjs` (21; unit values on screen the same as the engine in Node, row numbers, sources in the Excel Info sheet).

**BC03, Sectional Volume link (17 Nov 2026; `core/derived.js`, version 1.0.0-beta.2).** No new formula. The tree volumes computed by Sectional Volume with one method (outside bark `v_ob`, inside bark `v_ib`, m³) are written **as they are** from the engine's result (without rounding or unit change) to a separate `forest-derived/1` record; the record is bound to the canonical SHA-256 (§20) of the confirmed package and the package does not change (user decision, 17 Nov 2026: separate derived record; PROJECT §4b). Trees are matched by the plot × tree × period key; stem trees with no counterpart in the package are listed as `unmatched`, keys that fall on more than one package tree as `ambiguous` (with their rows), and are not written. The Biomass tool reads the record only if its fingerprint is the same as that of the package in use; if the bark side of the volume (the `v_ob`/`v_ib` read) does not match the side of the factors, no calculation is done (BC01 rule). An unknown schema version is rejected (`derived.version`); packages without transferred volumes are read as before. The "both" option computes the same trees in two ways and gives the results in separate rows (`bothApproaches`); there is no averaging or merging, the difference is not an error measure (the definitions differ). Test: `node tests/biomass/test_bc03.mjs` (22; the Sectional Volume engine's sample table result is bit-identical by `Object.is` through the record, the JSON round trip and the structured clone, up to the V read by Biomass; fingerprint, version and type checks; separate rows), browser `node tests/biomass/e2e_bc03.mjs` (17; transfer between the two pages, stored values bit-identical with Sectional Volume's own JSON output and with V in the Biomass Excel, old volumes not used for another package).

**BC-02/a1, additive component system (17 Nov 2026; `assets/lab/biomass/sur.js`, version 1.0.0-beta.4; user decisions: scope "fitting: additive system", sources from the user). Source [B]:** Parresol, B.R. 1999. Assessing tree and stand biomass: a review with examples and critical comparisons. *Forest Science* 45: 573–593 (full text uploaded by the user, 17 Nov 2026; §1.1.3–1.1.5, Equations 11–16 and 23, 31–35, Tables 1, 2 and 4 read). Nonlinear systems: BC-02/a2 below (Parresol 2001).
- **Forms:** each component B = b0 + Σ b_j·term_j, terms from a subset of {D, D², D²H, H} (the constant always). 15 forms registered as `biomass.lt.<terms>` (e.g. `lt.d2h.h`), coefficients b0, b1, …; the total-tree equation contains all terms in the components (Parresol's procedure 3, Equation 23: "the total-tree regression is a function of all independent variables used").
- **Additivity constraints:** b_total,0 = Σ b_i,0 and for each term τ b_total,τ = Σ_{i containing τ} b_i,τ (the general form of b40 = b10 + b20 + b30, b41 = b11 + b21, b42 = b31, b43 = b32 in Parresol's example). If there is no total column, total = sum of the components.
- **Weight (optional, per equation):** σ²_t = σ²·X_t^k (Equations 11–12, a single explanatory X ∈ {D, D², D²H, H}); k is given or estimated as the slope of the regression ln e²_t = a₁ + k·ln X_t from separate OLS residuals (Equation 15). The data are weighted by 1/√(X^k). There are no compound variance functions such as the additional exp(−kH²) multiplier in Parresol's crown equation.
- **Estimation (Equations 31–35):** separate (weighted) least squares residuals e_i; σ̂_ij = e_iᵀe_j / √((T − K_i)(T − K_j)); unconstrained SUR β̂ = [Xᵀ(Σ̂⁻¹⊗I)X]⁻¹Xᵀ(Σ̂⁻¹⊗I)y; constrained β* = β̂ + CRᵀ(RCRᵀ)⁻¹(r − Rβ̂), C = [Xᵀ(Σ̂⁻¹⊗I)X]⁻¹; Cov(β*) = C − CRᵀ(RCRᵀ)⁻¹RC (the text of Equation 35 is illegible in the PDF; this form reproduces the standard errors of Table 4 to the printed digits). No iteration (a single step as in Parresol's example).
- **Results:** coefficients and SEs of the constrained SUR and of the separate fit; on the kg scale Se = √(SSE/(n − K)), bias (mean y − ŷ), FI = 1 − SSE/SST (the R² form of the "fit index" in Parresol §1.1.2); residual correlations between equations. Trees with missing/invalid values are excluded with their rows.
- **Saving:** each equation as a `model-coef/1` set (`origin: 'fitted'`), `meta.cov` = the constrained covariance block of that equation, in unweighted equations `meta.rse` = Se; validity range the D (and if needed H) range of the sample; in `fit` the method, system id, n, weight, Σ̂, correlations. Covariances between equations are not written to the set; the BC04 uncertainty treats the sets as independent (note). If the total set is selected in BC02 as the "separate total equation", its difference from the component sum is zero.
- **Verification:** `node tests/biomass/test_bc02s.mjs` (19): with the 39 willow oaks of Parresol 1999 Table 1 (`tests/ref/parresol1999_willow.json`), the RSUR and WRSUR coefficients and standard errors of Table 4 agree at all printed digits; the example prediction in the paper (D = 30 cm, H = 18 m, LCL = 10 m → 583.8 kg). In WRSUR the exponent of the crown weight is illegible in the PDF text; the value 1.646 was found by matching b30 and gives all eleven values (noted in the test). Also with `tests/ref/make_biomass_bc02s.py` (standard library only, written separately) the estimated exponent, coefficients, SE and Σ̂; browser `node tests/biomass/e2e_bc02s.mjs` (10).

**BC-02/a2, nonlinear additive system (17 Nov 2026; `assets/lab/biomass/nsur.js`, version 1.0.0-beta.5; on the same card "System: nonlinear"). Source [B]:** Parresol, B.R. 2001. Additivity of nonlinear biomass equations. *Canadian Journal of Forest Research* 31: 865–878 (full text uploaded by the user, 17 Nov 2026; Equations 1–33, Tables 1–4 and the D = 20 cm, H = 17 m examples read).
- **Forms:** components from the registered power forms: a·(D²H)^b (`pow.d2h`), a·D^b (`pow.d`), a·D^b·H^c (`pow.dh`) (Parresol's Equation 15). The total equation is the sum of the component functions with the same coefficients (procedure 2, Equations 14, 18); additivity is ensured by structure, not by constraint, and the total has no separate coefficients.
- **Error structure (two options):** (1) additive error y = f(x, β) + ε, weight σ²_t = σ²·ψ_t, ψ_t = exp(g_tᵀθ) (Equations 2–3); g menu: ln D, ln H, ln D²H, ln(D·H) (ψ = X^θ), [ln D, H] and [ln D, H²] (ψ = D^θ1·e^(θ2·H) and D^θ1·e^(θ2·H²)); θ is given or estimated by OLS of ln e²_t = ln σ² + g_tᵀθ from the residuals of the unweighted fit (Equations 4–5); the θ of the total from residuals computed from the sum of the unweighted component fits (as in Equation 19). (2) Logarithmic scale (Example 2, Equation 32): all equations on the ln scale, ln y_total = ln(Σ f_i), ψ = 1; prediction exp(ln f), no back-transformation correction (as in the paper; the 264.6 kg of Example 2 is exp(5.5782)).
- **Separate fitting (EGNLS and procedure 1):** starting values by OLS on the ln scale (as in the paper), Gauss–Newton with step halving (Equations 7–8), cov(b) = σ̂²[ZᵀΨ⁻¹Z]⁻¹, σ̂² = S(b)/(T − K) (Equations 9a–b). The "separate" row of the total is the sum of the component fits (procedure 1, Equation 12).
- **NSUR (Equations 21–26):** σ̂_ij = e_iᵀΔ_iΔ_j e_j / √((T − K_i)(T − K_j)) from weighted single-equation residuals; the residual of the total from the sum of the component fits, K_total = number of coefficients in the system (this detail is not written in the paper; only this choice gives σ̂_44 = 8.46 × 10⁻⁷); Gauss–Newton iteration β_{n+1} = β_n + l_n[FᵀΔ(Σ̂⁻¹⊗I)ΔF]⁻¹FᵀΔ(Σ̂⁻¹⊗I)Δ(y − f) (Equation 24), step halving on R(β); Σ̂_b (Equation 25), σ̂²_NSUR = R(b)/(MT − K) (Equation 26). Σ̂ is not re-estimated (a single step as in the paper's example).
- **Intervals (Equations 27–28):** S² = f_iᵀΣ̂_b f_i; for the mean ŷ ± t·S, for a new tree ŷ ± t·√(S² + σ̂²_NSUR·σ̂_ii·ψ_i). t: as a tool rule Student t, MT − K df, 95% (the paper uses t = 2; the test with t = 2). On the logarithmic scale the interval is built on the ln scale and transformed back with exp (not symmetric, Equation 33).
- **Edge case (tool rule):** with additive error, no weights at all and total = sum of the components, the residual of the total is the sum of the component residuals and Σ̂ is singular; no fit is made, and weights or the logarithmic scale are suggested to the user. On the logarithmic scale trees with mass 0 are excluded.
- **Saving:** only component sets, with the registered power forms (`biomass.pow.*`, coefficients a, b, c = NSUR), `meta.cov` = NSUR covariance block (`rse` is not written: in a weighted system the residual variance σ̂²_NSUR·σ̂_ii·ψ_t varies by tree); in `fit` the method, θ, Σ̂, correlations, σ̂²_NSUR. The total is obtained by summing the sets in the mapping.
- **Misprints in the source (note):** (1) in Table 2 the crown weight is printed as D^7.322·e^(−0.006185·H); with these data Equation 4 gives θ2 = −0.2015 for H, and 7.32207 and −0.0061840 with H², and the crown coefficients, SEs and σ̂² = 3.9417 × 10⁻⁷ of Table 2 come out only with H²: the weight is read as D^7.322·e^(−0.006185·H²). (2) In the line of Equation 30, S² = 0.44 is printed for the total (a repeat of that of the bark); Equation 31 uses 9.77 and the calculation gives 9.77. (3) The last digit of the unweighted crown coefficients in Table 2 (0.012317, −0.33691) differs by one unit from the least-squares optimum (0.0123175, −0.336893) (SAS convergence). (4) The θ of the total is computed as 3.4520, printed 3.450.
- **Discrepancy from Example 2 (note):** the logarithmic column of Table 3 could not be reproduced to the printed digits: the tool builds Σ̂ from the separate fit residuals on the ln scale (the rule in Example 1) and finds b31 = 0.0355, b32 = 3.923, b33 = −1.620 (printed 0.035861, 3.8704, −1.5717; deviation ≤ 3%), ln ŷ_total = 5.5751 (printed 5.5782); σ̂_44 = 7.74 × 10⁻³ (printed 6.954 × 10⁻³). Pooled OLS, iterated Σ̂ and n-divisor variants did not give the printed values either; the paper does not state how Σ̂ was built in Example 2.
- **Verification:** `node tests/biomass/test_bc02n.mjs` (44): with the 40 slash pine trees of Table 1 (`tests/ref/parresol2001_slashpine.json`; in each row bole + bark + crown = tree) the unweighted and weighted coefficients, SEs and σ̂² of Table 2 to the printed digits (unweighted crown 1e-4 relative); θs 2.114, 3.715, 7.322/−0.006185 (H²), 3.450 (±0.003); procedure 1 example 186.4 + 34.7 + 47.0 = 268.1 kg and var(ŷ_bole) = 8.46; Table 3 weighted NSUR: bole and bark coefficients and seven SEs to the printed digits, crown coefficients within 0.03% (with the printed weights); σ̂_44 = 8.46 × 10⁻⁷, σ̂²_NSUR = 0.933; Table 4 FI 0.984/0.961/0.909/0.988; bark 34.7 kg, S² 0.44, ±1.3; total 264.2 kg, S² 9.77, ±6.3 and ±41.8, [222.4, 306.0]. Independent reference `tests/ref/make_biomass_bc02n.py` (NumPy, written separately): weighted system with estimated θ, ln scale and a second case with a total column/unweighted components/D^θ1·e^(θ2·H) weight (θ, EGNLS, Σ̂, NSUR coefficients and SE, σ̂²_NSUR, Se/bias/FI, intervals). `tests/biomass/e2e_bc02n.mjs` (17).

**BC04, Monte Carlo uncertainty (17 Nov 2026; `assets/lab/biomass/mc.js`, version 1.0.0-beta.3; PLATFORM_PLAN BC-06). Sources read (17 Nov 2026, full texts uploaded by the user) [B]:**
- Réjou-Méchain, M., Tanguy, A., Piponiot, C., Chave, J., Hérault, B. 2017. *Methods in Ecology and Evolution* 8: 1163–1167, "Final propagation" (p. 1166): repeated steps: "Allometric model parameters are set up randomly picking up a single vector of posterior parameters applied simultaneously to all the trees of a stand (i.e. corresponding to a systematic error)" and "A single error term is added for all trees independently from the distribution N(0, RSEp)"; the replicates are summed per stand, "mean, median and 95% credibility interval" are reported. The scheme in the tool (one common coefficient draw per set, independent residual per tree, summary of the stand total) is the same as this [B].
- Chave, J., Condit, R., Aguilar, S., Hernandez, A., Lao, S., Perez, R. 2004. *Phil. Trans. R. Soc. B* 359: 409–420, §2(a) (p. 410): ln(AGB) = ln(f) + ξ, ξ normal, s.d. σ; "the expectation of exp(ξ) is ⟨exp(ξ)⟩ = exp(σ²/2)", CF = exp(σ²/2), ⟨AGB⟩ = CF × f; tree-level uncertainty "averages out at the stand level". This is the justification for not applying a correction factor additionally when the residual is simulated on the ln scale (the mean of the replicates converges to exp(σ²/2)·f; `test_bc04` verifies this numerically) [B]. Chave 2004 handles coefficient uncertainty not with a covariance but with resampling from the calibration sample ("rarefaction"); this method is not in the tool.
- **Point of departure from the sources (note):** BIOMASS draws coefficients by selecting rows from MCMC posterior samples (each row with its own RSE); the tool draws θ ~ N(θ̂, Σ) with the covariance given in the user's source and uses a single rse. This is a normal approximation (TOOLS_SPEC BC04: "normal error" assumption) and is written in the result as an assumption. As in Réjou-Méchain 2017, the median was also added to the summaries.
Read previously: the source code and help page of `AGBmonteCarlo` in the BIOMASS package (rdrr.io copy, 17 Nov 2026; the version number could not be read): the coefficients are drawn once per replicate (one row from the posterior table) and applied to all trees, the residual is added on the ln scale with a separate `rnorm(sd = RSE)` for each tree and replicate, the back-transformation is done with `exp()` **without a correction factor**, and the mean, median, SD and 2.5–97.5% percentiles of the stand total are given. Implementation in the tool:
- Only what is given in the set's source is simulated (user decision: "compute what is there, label it"): coefficient covariance `meta.cov` (matrix in coefficient order) and residual standard error `meta.rse` (on the equation's scale: kg in linear forms, that scale in ln/log₁₀ forms). A set with neither is held at its point value and listed; if no set in use has the information, **"no uncertainty information"**, no calculation is done. The covariance is checked for shape, positive diagonal, symmetry and Cholesky (positive definiteness); if it fails, no calculation is done and it is written with the set name.
- Replicate b: θ_b = θ̂ + L·z (L lower Cholesky factor, z standard normal), one draw per set, common to all trees using that set (as in BIOMASS; BIOMASS selects a row from the posterior table, the tool draws a multivariate normal from the covariance — difference noted). Residual ε ~ N(0, rse²) independent for each tree × component, added on the equation's scale. In log forms with a simulated residual no correction factor is applied (the same as BIOMASS; the residual already carries the mean of exp(ε)); if there is no residual, the set's own correction is applied as in the point prediction. In linear forms negative draws are not truncated, their number is written.
- Random numbers: a single stream mulberry32(seed) (`core/stats.js` `rng`), z = Φ⁻¹(u + 2⁻³³); order: first, for b = 1…B, the coefficients of the sets in id order, then the tree × component × replicate residuals in package order. The same seed gives the same numbers.
- Summary: per tree (kg) and per plot × period (Mg/ha; B, C, CO₂) point value, mean, SD (n − 1), CV, percentile interval (sample percentile type 7; level 90%/95%/99%). No interval in a unit with a tree that cannot be computed (point total rule). Tool rules (written in the result): default 1000 replicates (100–20000), seed 20261117, 95%.
- Assumptions (written in the result): coefficient errors multivariate normal, residuals normal; independence between trees, components and sets. Diameter/height measurement errors and the uncertainty of the carbon fraction and of the volume conversion factors are not included ("no uncertainty information" in the volume path).
- Test: `node tests/biomass/test_bc04.mjs` (21; reference `tests/ref/make_biomass_bc04.py`, standard library only, the same generator and draw order, tree and unit summaries 1e-9; closed forms: in a linear form with coefficients only → Var = xᵀΣx and with the common draw the unit variance (Σx)ᵀΣ(Σx); in an ln form with residual only → mean exp(η + σ²/2), limits exp(η ± 1.96σ), independence between trees; statuses), browser `node tests/biomass/e2e_bc04.mjs` (13).

Test: `node tests/biomass/test_bc01.mjs` (25); reference `tests/ref/make_biomass_bc01.py` (plain Python; random coefficients belonging to no species; log₁₀ forms converted to the ln scale; stand totals with separate loops; TOOLS_SPEC §8.4 constants).

Sources: IPCC 2006. *2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories*, Volume 4 (AFOLU), Chapter 2 (Generic Methodologies Applicable to Multiple Land-Use Categories), Equation 2.8, §2.2.3. IGES, Japan. **[B]** · Baskerville, G.L. 1972. Use of logarithmic regression in the estimation of plant biomass. *Canadian Journal of Forest Research* 2: 49–53. doi:10.1139/x72-009. **[A]**, cited in: Chave, J., Réjou-Méchain, M., Búrquez, A. et al. 2014. Improved allometric models to estimate the aboveground biomass of tropical trees. *Global Change Biology* 20: 3177–3190. doi:10.1111/gcb.12629 [read] and Baia, A.L.P., Nascimento, H.E.M., Guedes, M., Hilário, R., Toledo, J.J. 2025. Tree height-diameter allometry and implications for biomass estimates in Northeastern Amazonian forests. *PeerJ* 13: e18974. doi:10.7717/peerj.18974 [read]. · Sprugel, D.G. 1983. Correcting for bias in log-transformed allometric equations. *Ecology* 64: 209–210. **[B]** (PDF provided by the user, read on 22 Nov 2026; the log₁₀ conversion SEE·ln 10 is applied since 1.0.0-beta.6).

## 23. Tree measurement (`assets/lab/tree/`; PLATFORM_PLAN TM)

Sources (17 Nov 2026, two books uploaded by the user; TM decision: once the book arrives, definition formulas also from the book [B]):
- **[B]** Hamilton, G.J. 1975. *Forest Mensuration Handbook.* Forestry Commission Booklet 39. HMSO, London (3rd edition 1988).
- **[B]** Graves, H.S. 1906. *Forest Mensuration.* John Wiley & Sons, New York (public domain; text from the Internet Archive scan `forestmensuratio00grav`, the same as the PDF in Drive).
- **[B]** Kershaw, J.A., Ducey, M.J., Beers, T.W., Husch, B. 2016. *Forest Mensuration*, 5th edition. Wiley-Blackwell (17 Nov 2026: the pages set aside by the user were read, pp. 125–131, 236–246, 263–266, Appendix A.2–A.3; the whole book is cut off at p. ~67 in the reading tool).

### TM-01/a1: height (17 Nov 2026; `tree/height.js`)
- **Tangent (percent) method**, horizontal distance D: the part above the eye D·tan α_top, the part to the base D·tan α_base; if the base is below the eye they are added, if above, subtracted (Graves §73–74 clinometer and Abney; §67 Figure 19; Hamilton Figure 19 AC = OC·tan a1). Here the angles are from the horizontal, upward positive; the two cases combine into one formula: **H = D·(tan α_top − tan α_base)**. Percent scale: tan = p/100 (Graves §73: percent is the ratio of the rise to the horizontal distance).
- **Distance along the line of sight** (rangefinder): Hamilton Figure 19: OC = OB·cos a2, BC = OB·sin a2, AC = OC·tan a1, AB = AC ± BC; note to §3: if the distance is measured to another point P on the stem, OC = OP·cos(angle of P). Graves §72 Brandis: BC = AC·sin(u + l)/cos u. The two are the same expression; in the test both are checked independently.
- **Distance on sloping ground:** horizontal distance = slope distance·cos(slope angle) (the same relation as OC = OB·cos a2 in Hamilton Figure 19).
- **Similar triangles** (Graves §65): shadow method H = tree shadow × pole length / pole shadow; two-pole method H = bc·AC/Ac (the book's example 6 × 30 / 4 = 45).
- **Warnings (from the sources):** if the distance is smaller than the height (Graves §76: as much as the height, more rather than less; Hamilton §5: 1–1½ times the height); a tool-rule warning if the sight to the top is steeper than 60° (no number in the source; with a steep sight the top error grows, Hamilton §5 "error large when very close").
- **Definitions:** height is the vertical distance between the tip and the base; on a slope the base is the ground level on the uphill side of the tree (Hamilton §6; Graves §76 recommends standing above the base on a slope). Mean and range of repeated readings (Hamilton §7).
- **Not implemented:** the stem length of a leaning tree (Graves §76 says it is to be computed by trigonometry but gives no formula); instrument tables and instrument setups (Faustmann, Christen, Klaussner, Weise…); practical advice such as choosing the top in the leaf mass is written only in the guide.
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_height_tm01.py` (angles by atan2 from the geometry of a tree of known height and an observer, percent readings, distances to the eye and to a stem point; Brandis form with mpmath 50 digits). `tests/tree/test_tm01.mjs` 696/696: in 200 cases the height with degrees, percent, distance to the base and distance to a stem point worst 9.6e−16 relative; the same as the Brandis form; Graves's two-pole example 45; shadow, horizontal distance (3-4-5), 45° = 100%, ordering, range and unit errors.

### TM-02/a1: diameter and basal area (17 Nov 2026; `tree/diameter.js`)
- **Circumference ↔ diameter** d = c/π; **basal area** g = π/4·d² (d converted to m, g m²) (Hamilton V §8 circle; Graves §43 diameter tape).
- **Multiple readings:** two perpendicular diameters or several diameters are averaged arithmetically (Graves §114, §143); as an option the two diameters are taken as the axes of an ellipse, area π·a·b = π/4·d₁·d₂, equivalent diameter √(d₁d₂) (Hamilton V §8 ellipse). A circumference reading is first converted to a diameter.
- **Rounding:** none / down to the whole centimetre (Hamilton II §2, classification tape; a value on the boundary stays in the upper class) / millimetre. Units: cm, mm, m, inch (1 in = 2.54 cm, Hamilton V §9); calculation and output metric (P5).
- **Tree list:** N, ΣG, arithmetic mean diameter and the diameter of the tree of mean basal area (Graves §127: B̄ = Σbᵢnᵢ/N, the diameter of B̄).
- **Measurement rules** (Hamilton II §2 a: 1.3 m, tape taut and perpendicular to the stem, uphill side on a slope, underside of a leaning tree, on ploughed ground from the root collar or the ground whichever is higher, at a swelling the narrowest point below it, fork below 1.3 m: each stem a separate tree, fork at 1.3 m: one tree, below the fork; Graves §42 caliper perpendicular to the stem, at a swelling above or below or the mean of the two; a tape generally measures larger than a caliper) are written in the interface and the guide; they are not calculations.
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_tree_tm02_06.py` (40 random tree lists, mpmath 40 digits); `tests/tree/test_tm02_06.mjs`.

### TM-06/a1: form factor, form exponent, form quotient (17 Nov 2026; `tree/form.js`)
- **Form factor** F = V/(B·H) (Graves §91): breast-height (B at 1.3 m), absolute (B at the base), absolute above breast height (§95: volume above 1.3 m, B at 1.3 m, H − 1.3), normal (§96 Pressler: B at H/20). Whichever volume is given (merchantable, stem, tree), it is that form factor. V = B·H·F (§92); form height F·H (§98). F > 1 is warned about.
- **Form exponent** (§97): y² = p·xʳ (x from the tip); r = 2(log y₁ − log y₂)/(log x₁ − log x₂); r = 0 cylinder, 1 paraboloid, 2 cone, 3 neiloid; absolute form factor 1/(r + 1); breast-height form factor (1/(r + 1))·(H/(H − 1.3))ʳ (Graves writes 4½ ft).
  - **Arithmetic error in the book's example:** for 18 and 15 inches at 61 and 45 ft from the tip, the book writes log 1.25 = 0.086910 and finds r = 1.35. Correct is log 1.25 = 0.096910 and r = 1.199. The printed page was verified from the image (Internet Archive scan p. 184, a file byte-for-byte identical to the PDF in Drive). The tool applies the formula; the test documents both values.
- **Form quotient** (§100): q = d/D (d at the middle of the stem, D at breast height); F = q − c, species-specific c from the user (the book's values for European species are not embedded, K1).
- **Not implemented:** the approximate methods in §99 (Philipp, Strzelecki, Nossek, Schaal) and the §83 Pressler formula (the printed formula is not legible in the copy read).
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_tree_tm02_06.py` (60 solids of revolution d² = D²(x/H)ʳ, random r and H, volume scipy quad, four form factors and r from two diameters); `tests/tree/test_tm02_06.mjs` 297/297: four form factors, 1/(r + 1), breast-height formula, exponent from two diameters (1e−11), V = BHF, solid names, Graves's example and its error, form quotient, diameter/basal area/unit/rounding, 40 tree lists (1e−13).

### Tree Measurement page (17 Nov 2026; `tree-measurement.php`, `assets/lab/tree/ui.js`, 1.0.0-beta.1; TM-01, TM-02, TM-06)
A single page, three tabs (TM decision); results update as the fields change, the session is stored in the browser. **Height:** method (tangent, rangefinder to the base or to a stem point, shadow, two poles), angle unit (degree/percent), converting a distance measured on sloping ground to horizontal, height and note (issues and warnings) per row, mean and range, rules text. **Diameter and basal area:** single stem (reading rows: diameter/circumference, unit; arithmetic or ellipse; rounding), tree list (table/paste, diameter or circumference column, optional count column, unit, decimal separator; unreadable rows with their numbers; simulated sample `tree/ornek/ornek-capler.csv`), list of Hamilton/Graves measurement rules. **Form factor:** form factor (four kinds), form exponent (breast-height form factor if the height is entered), form quotient (constant c from the user). Download: Excel (Info, Height, Stem, Tree list, Form) and JSON run record (DI-07; fingerprint of the tree list file). Drawing on the home page card (`inc/hero.php` `hero_tree`).
- **Verification:** `tests/tree/e2e.mjs` 26/26 (in source and bundled mode): heights on screen (tangent, decimal comma, percent + sloping ground, rangefinder, two poles 45), warning and issue texts, row deletion; circumference → diameter, rounding down, mean of two diameters, ellipse; in the sample list N, ΣG, mean diameter and dg the same as the engine; form factor, the exponent of Graves's example 1.1987 and the breast-height form factor, form quotient; Excel sheets; version, sources and the file's SHA-256 in the JSON record; session, English, phone; guide (`test_guide` 238), site e2e 100.

### TM-05/a1: tree age and increment (17 Nov 2026; `tree/age.js`)
Sources: Graves 1906 chapters XV–XVI [B]; van Laar, A., Akça, A. 2007. *Forest Mensuration.* Springer, chapter 4 §1.1 [B] (uploaded 17 Nov 2026; chapter 9 "Growth and yield" could not be read because the Drive reading tool cuts the text at page 163 of the PDF).
- **Tree age:** number of rings at the counting height + the time to reach that height (Graves §137–138; van Laar and Akça 4.1.1). The time comes from the user's own seedling study; the site gives no value (K1). A false ring increases the age, a missing ring decreases it (van Laar and Akça; cross-dating in the guide).
- **Lorey's economic age** (§141): rings outside the suppressed core + the mean age of the cores at that diameter (the book's example 70 + 14 = 84).
- **Tree without rings** (§139): age = diameter / mean annual diameter increment (example 40 inches, 1 inch in 4 years → 160 years).
- **Mean stand age** (§140): arithmetic mean of the sample trees; Σnᵢaᵢ/N; basal-area-weighted Σbᵢaᵢ/B; Smalian's volume formula A = Σvᵢ / Σ(vᵢ/aᵢ) (total volume / total mean annual increment; the most correct according to the book).
- **Increment** (§142): current, periodic, mean annual (total / age), periodic annual ((now − before)/years); increment percent P = 100·a/D.
- **Increment percent over n years** (§172): principal as past value 100(V − v)/(v·n), as present value 100(V − v)/(V·n) (book: gives an underestimate) or their mean (Pressler) 200/n·(V − v)/(V + v); always present < mean < past. **Pressler, standing tree** (§174): 200/n·(D² − d²)/(D² + d²), d the diameter n years ago from an increment borer; the same formula with relative diameter q = D/a, a = D − d: 200/n·(2q − 1)/(q² + (q − 1)²) (Pressler's tables; the printed form in the book is illegible in the scan, the equality of the two forms is shown in the test).
- **Increment core** (§138, §150): ring widths from the bark inward (mm), present diameter and optional double bark thickness (to get down to the inside-bark diameter), time to reach breast height: inside-bark diameter k years ago = D_inside-bark − 2·(sum of the outermost k widths) (diameter increment is twice the radial increment, §150); age at that time = present age − k; ten-year table. Warnings: the core is longer than the radius, falls short of the pith by more than 2%, or does not reach the pith (then the ring count and the age are lower bounds).
- **Not implemented:** the table of class differences from stump diameter to DBH (§150, species- and data-specific; meaningless without a user table), Pressler's tree classes with changing height and form factor (continuation of §174), value increment at compound interest.
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_age_tm05.py` (exact arithmetic with Python `fractions`: 30 stands × four mean ages, 40 increment pairs × three principals and Pressler, 25 increment cores); `tests/tree/test_tm05.mjs` 259/259: all 1e−13 (Pressler relative diameter 1e−10), Graves's two examples (84 and 160), the order present < mean < past, warnings and errors.

### Tree Measurement page, 1.0.0-beta.2: "Age and increment" tab (17 Nov 2026; TM-05; user decision: a fourth tab instead of a separate page)
Tree age, economic age, age of a tree without rings; increment core (widths separated by space, line, ; or tab, decimal point or comma; inside-bark diameter and age for every year, table every ten years, all years on the Excel "Core" sheet; for widths exceeding the radius no negative diameter is shown, "–" and a warning; periodic annual diameter increment and Pressler percent for the selected n years); the four mean ages of the stand (group table); increment percent with three principals and mean annual increment. "Age and increment" and "Core" sheets in Excel, `age` in the JSON record. Because the method set changed, version 1.0.0-beta.2 (K4).
- **Verification:** `tests/tree/e2e.mjs` 34/34 (source and bundled): age 58, economic age 84, rejection of a non-integer ring count, increment core (mixed separators and decimal comma; 20 rings, age 26, inside bark 11 cm; warning for not reaching the pith; ten-year table and Pressler the same as the engine), four mean ages, three increment percents, Excel sheets (all years), session, English; guide 256 (new check: sections not nested).
- **Guide repair:** in the merge on main (`9c8974b`) the `</ul></section>` closing of the Tree Measurement section had been dropped; the Biomass and Carbon section remained inside it (TR and EN). The closing was restored; `tests/site/test_guide.mjs` now checks that each section closes before the next one.


### TM-03/a1: bark thickness and inside-bark diameter (17 Nov 2026; `tree/bark.js`)
Sources: Hamilton 1975, Part V §5 Bark (p. 147), paragraphs 3–4, 8, 11 and Part III Procedure 1 (mid-diameter × length) [B]; Graves 1906 §42 (pp. 77–78) [B]; van Laar and Akça 2007, 4.1.6 Bark thickness and chapter 3 "Bark thickness" (bark gauge) [B]. Meyer (1942), Zöhrer et al. (1973) and Oestlin (1963) were read from van Laar and Akça (the original publications were not read).
- **Definition of bark thickness:** the girth bark thickness, the mean distance between the plane of the tape over the bark and the plane of the tape under the bark at the same place; a bark gauge with an adequate plate gives approximately the same value (Hamilton ¶3). From this, with two girths B = (c_outside-bark − c_inside-bark)/(2π) (the radius difference of two circles). Several readings are averaged; in deeply fissured bark each reading is from the wood to the tangent of the caliper arm (Graves §42).
- **Inside-bark diameter** = outside-bark diameter − double bark thickness (Hamilton ¶8); from inside-bark to outside-bark diameter by adding the bark (Graves §42; Graves says measuring inside and adding is more accurate). Bark area = g_outside-bark − g_inside-bark (g = π/4·d²), its share as a percentage of the outside-bark area.
- **Bark volume:** mean bark cross-sectional area × length (¶4) or the difference of outside-bark and inside-bark volume (¶11); log volume mid-diameter × length (Procedure 1). In the tool, if a length is entered the diameters are taken as mid-diameters: V = g·L, bark volume = (g_outside-bark − g_inside-bark)·L (the two ways are the same).
- **Bark ratio** k = d_inside-bark/d_outside-bark (Meyer 1942), assumed constant within a stand (a linear relation between outside-bark and inside-bark diameter); its inverse 1/k (Zöhrer et al. 1973); estimation by the ratio of means k = Σd_inside-bark/Σd_outside-bark (van Laar and Akça 4.1.6). From sample trees also the least-squares line d_inside-bark = a + b·D (Meyer's linear relation), R² and residual standard error (n − 2); at least three trees and different diameters.
- **Coefficient set forms** (`model-coef/1`, tool `bark`; K1: the site contains no coefficients or tables such as Hamilton's bark tables): d_inside-bark = k·D; d_inside-bark = a + b·D; single or double bark thickness (mm) = a + b·D (linear relation between bark thickness and diameter, van Laar and Akça); ln(bark, mm) = a + b·ln D (Oestlin 1963, cited in van Laar and Akça), with the set's correction factor in the back-transformation (Baskerville [A], §22; user or Snowdon factor). D cm. If the result is not 0 < d_inside-bark ≤ D or the bark comes out negative, the calculation is rejected (not clipped); warning outside the validity range.
- **Not implemented:** bark models along the stem (Johnson et al. 1987, Cao–Pepper, Laasasenaho; sources not read), site index and habitat variables of relative bark thickness (Johnson et al.), bark weight (Deetlefs 1957), Example 4.2 of van Laar and Akça (P. patula coefficients; K1).
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_bark_tm03.py` (mpmath 40 digits: 80 points, 40 girth pairs; numpy.polyfit: 30 sample tree sets; six forms × 10 equations); `tests/tree/test_tm03.mjs` 438/438: 1e−11 (fitted line a 1e−8), hand cases (30 cm, 10 mm → 28 cm; 40/36 cm → bark share 19%; rejection cases; a set without a source is not applied).

### Tree Measurement page, 1.0.0-beta.3: "Bark" tab (17 Nov 2026; TM-03; user decision: a tab instead of a separate page, scope measurement + k + user equation)
Bark at a point (bark gauge readings, two girths or from inside), bark volume with an optional log length; k and line from sample trees (by typing or pasting two columns); applying the inside-bark diameter to a single diameter and to the tree list in the "Diameter and basal area" tab (manual k, k from the sample, line from the sample, coefficient set); a shared coefficient editor on the page (the sets are also in the Coefficient Library, tool "Tree Measurement: bark"). Excel "Bark" sheet (every tree of the list), `bark` in the JSON record. Because the method set changed, 1.0.0-beta.3 (K4).
- **Verification:** `tests/tree/e2e.mjs` 46/46 (source and bundled): readings, volume, two girths, rejection, sample trees (pasted row and excluded row), 30 → 27 cm with k = 0.9, tree list totals, line, ln 2B set, Excel, session; guide 309; coefficient library 21.

### TM-04/a1: crown metrics (17 Nov 2026; `tree/crown.js`)
Sources: van Laar and Akça 2007, 4.1.7 Tree crown and foliage (pp. 80–82) [B]; Kershaw et al. 2016, §5.5.1–5.5.3, §5.5.5 (pp. 125–129), §8.6.1 (pp. 236–237), Appendix Table A.2 [B]; Hamilton 1975, Part V §8 geometry formulas (p. 190) [B]. Gregoire and Valentine (1995), Röhle et al. (1985), Schreuder et al. (1993) were read from Kershaw or van Laar (the original publications were not read).
- **Crown radius and diameter:** radius from the stem centre to the outer edge of the crown (usually at the widest point), crown diameter twice the radius; radii from 4 or more directions (4–8 for a stand, 8–16 for a single tree; Röhle) (van Laar). Tool: mean radius and crown diameter 2·r̄; warning with fewer than 4 radii.
- **Crown projection area:** if the quadratic mean of radii taken from random or regular directions is taken as the radius of the equivalent circle, the area π·r_q² is unbiased regardless of crown shape (Kershaw §5.5.2, Gregoire and Valentine 1995). From two diameters: a circle with the mean diameter (mean of the largest and smallest diameter, two perpendicular diameters, or the largest diameter and the diameter perpendicular to it) or an ellipse π·a·b with the diameters as axes (Kershaw: like caliper diameter measurement, elliptical shape assumption; Appendix A.2).
- **Crown length** = tree height − height of the crown base (Kershaw §5.5.1); **crown ratio** = crown length / height · 100 (van Laar; Kershaw §5.5.5). Definitions of the crown base vary in the sources (Curtis and Reukema: the first whorl with live foliage in at least three of four quadrants; Germany: the whorl with at least three live branches; van Laar 1969, 1972); the tool leaves the definition to the user and writes it in the guide. **Crown diameter / DBH** (Kershaw §5.5.2, in the same unit).
- **Crown surface and volume** (Hamilton §8; cone the same as Kershaw 5.18–5.19: S = π·d_b·L/2, V = π·d_b²·l/12): cylinder V = πr²L, lateral surface 2πrL; cone V = πW²L/12, lateral surface πrs (s slant length); paraboloid V = πr²L/2, surface 2πr/(12L²)·((r² + 4L²)^{3/2} − r³) + πr², outer surface by subtracting the base πr². **The paraboloid formula printed by van Laar and Akça on p. 82 lacks the "− r³" term** (verified with the image of the printed page; without the term the surface goes to infinity as the crown length goes to zero); Hamilton's is used and is verified in the test by numerical integration of the surface of revolution. To avoid loss of significance in flat crowns, the same expression is computed in the form 2πr/3·(3r⁴ + 12r²L² + 16L⁴)/((r² + 4L²)^{3/2} + r³).
- **Crown cover** (Kershaw §8.6.1): total crown projection area of the trees / ground area (m²/ha or unitless); can exceed 1 when crowns overlap; it differs from canopy cover (the share of ground covered) and canopy closure (the share of sky covered from a point). Tool: the crown diameters in the plot are treated as circles.
- **Not implemented:** crown ratio regressions (Dyer 1987, Holdaway 1986; they require coefficients and their sources were not read), crown polygon area from polar coordinates (no formula in the source), leaf area, crown density and FIA ratings, canopy closure measurement (densiometer), CCF (see below).
- **CCF note (for SS-02):** Kershaw §8.9.4 (citing Krajicek et al. 1961): for open-grown trees CW = b₀ + b₁d, MCA = 100·(πCW²/4)/A (percentage of the area; 0.0078·CW² in SI), CCF = Σ nᵢ·MCAᵢ (per unit area), CCF = 100 is a closed canopy where the crowns just touch. van Laar and Akça (pp. 156–157) write CCF = Σ MCA/10000 (MCA in m²; as a ratio, 1/100 of Kershaw's). The scale difference is thus established by two sources; CCF was added to Stand Structure (§16).
- **Verification:** `tests/ref/make_crown_tm04.py` (volume and lateral surface of solids of revolution with scipy `quad`, without using the closed formulas; ellipse area by integral; radii and cover with mpmath); `tests/tree/test_tm04.mjs` 145/145: volume 1e−11, surface 1e−9, that van Laar's printed form gives exactly πr⁴/(6L²) too much, that a flat paraboloid approaches the base area, rejection cases.

Crown projection (radii or two diameters), crown length and ratio (with optional DBH, crown diameter / DBH), crown surface and volume for three shapes, crown cover on a plot; Excel "Crown", `crown` in the JSON record. Because the method set changed, 1.0.0-beta.4 (K4).
